quoise. Like most other jewels it looks best with white, ! but being an accommodating kind of stone it "swears" at very Few colours. Perhaps worst of all is the wrong blue, when put with the turquoise. That way "tooth- ache lies," for two keys of the same colour are more harrowing together than any diverse pair of hues. The most beautiful turquoises are a perfect blue with scarcely a trace of green about them. Turquoises how- ever are very sensitive stones and should never be donned when the wearer is out of health. It is ascertained fact that the ill-health of their wearer, turns the bluest stones green, probably as the result of acid in the system. The Duchess of Westminster, like her sister Princess Henry of Pleas has a great affection for tur- quoises, and possesses the largest flawless specimen owned by any private individual. The Toys of Ancient Times.—There is something fascinating in the toy or plaything which amused the child of two thousand years or more ago. The doll was then, as it is still, the most prized toy of childhood,and, how little childish tastes have changed may be seen from the collection of the toys of ancient Greece and Rome, which has been recently re-arranged in the British Museum. It includes beside dolls, and doll's furniture, rattles, discs and knuckle-bones with which games were played. When the child grew up,to the man,or woman,the toys were dedicated to deities,and deposited in the sanctearies,but if death came at a tender age, the children had their toys buried with them, and many have been found on the tombs where they were deposited so long ago. The Ostrich Plume Spoiled.—Possessors of long ostrich feathers that have decidedly seen their best days should take heart, and proceed to have the plumes dressed in a manner that will bring them quite up-to- date. lhe feather is robbed of its fronds, though the spines are allowed to remain, and these are made to resemble flue twigs, curving this way and that, in hope- less confusion. That a woman could be found who would pay for a handsome ostrich plume, and then have it so treated is almost past belief, bnt apparently "exclusive " dressers will go even to this length. Wrist Bags.—The wrist bags most sought after at the moment are those of a real, old-fashioned beadwork. so ' modish in this country a century ago. The work origin- ated in Germany, and such antique specimens as can be found are highly valued. The beads in the best examples are knitted in on the finest thread, or several strands of hair twisted together, and in many cases the work is beautiful, and colouring very fine. For those who cannot afford the genuine article, there are some wonderful and fairly cheap ircitatiens being offered, while many a clever knitter is doing the work herself, the expense in this instance being little more than that involved in having it lined and framed.

MADGE.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Deceased Wife's—Sister.—The Deceased Wife's sister Marriage Bill,has experienced many vicissitudes, but the chances of it becoming law this Session,are more favourable than on any of the numerous occasions it has Come before Parliament during the past seventy years. It has been passed by the House of Commons no fewer than eighteen times, only to come to grief in the House of Lords, but in 1896 it was even passed in the Upper Chamber, when one of the votes cast in its favour was that of the King, but for some reason or other it has always failed to pass through all the intricacies of Parliamentary procedure. These formalities enabled Opponents of the Bill to put many difficulties in its way, but, to some extent, these opportunities have been curtailed by new rules of procedure. The Colonies, almost without exception, sanction such marriages, and the Act of last year eave them legal recognition in the I United Kingdom, tis true that a large body of. Opinion in the Church of England. is antagonistic, but the great branches of the Christian Church genetally are m favour of legalisiug the marriages, and even the Church of Rome grants dispensations. The present Bill was passed in the House of Commons last week, by 263. votes to 34, and, although the Bishops remain irrecon- ciliable,Parl lament cannot any longer ignore the weight of public opinion in favour of legalising marriage with a deceased wife's sister. The Crucial Battle.—Marriage with a deceased wife's sister is largely a woman's question, and the failure of Parliament to deal with it, on anything like business- like principles, is naturally used as an argument in favour of giving women the franchise. Into this con- troversy one or two new features have entered.—Mrs. Fawcett, and Mrs. McLaren,representing the " constitu- tional " suffragists, have been refused a hearing at the Bar of the House of Commons, a refusal on the part of the Prime Alinister, which must tend to throw the movement more into the handy of the " militant " suffragists. Then there is the new counteracting force of the petitions got up by women who do not want votes. The crucial battle however will be fought out in the House of Commons on March 8, when Mr. Dickenson's Bill to give women the Parliamentary vote will come up for discussion. Attempts to get it "talked out "are not likely to succeed, but if it passes its second reading, as is expected, its ultimate success will greatly depend on the attitude of the Government towards it If it should be an unfavourable one, the "militaet " setlrsgists say that lively times are in store for the Ministry. Father t. buguan's New Campaign.—Father Vaughan, whose dissertations on the sins and follies of Society attracted so much attention last summer, is devoting his Lenten discourses to a new series of sermons on the same subject. Large and fashionable congregations crammed his church to overflowing, and he is again raising his voice against the gambling and cheating, the extravagance, the craze for putrid literature, and other vices attributed to that section of society commonly designated the "smart set." One result of the cam- paign last summer was to dub this section the "shocking set,' who, however, instead of showing any signs of reform, seem rather to glory in their notoriety. The Enphemism "Not at Home."—The question of the immorality of the conventional "not at home," when it is not literally true, has often caused searchings at heart, and Mary Jane has teen known to give notice rather than put such a strain on her conscience. It is said of Dr. Johnson, that when he did not want to be Interrupted he sometimes let his servant think he was out when he was at home. The servant, he said, would take a servants view of the matter, and if he became accustomed to telling a lie for his master, he would probably tell many lies for himself. Bishop Weldon, of Manchester, objects to the custom on higher grounds, but it is always understood that "not at home" has no reference to whether the mistress is in or out, but simply that visitors cannot be received. Again, we are accustomed to express regret in declining an invitation, when very often we are glad to be excused, and indeed social life would hardly be pleasant, or even tolerable, without its recognised eaphernisms. Spring Attire.—We grow more previous year by year. Once upon a time the fashionable woman never dretmed of wearing her new costumes before Eastertide.Now-a- days, so soon as the January sales are over, spring attire is the all absorbing topic, and mantra modish individual catches a severe cold because she insists upon mixing tm the seasons, according to her computation, and wears dainty spring clothes in a bitter east wind. The wise among us will be content with a new hat,veils,neckwear, petticoats, and little renovation, and wait a few weeks yet before thinking of donning spring costumes. Concerning Millirrery.—The shape of the moment is our old friend the sailor, with high crown and some- what narrow, choice the narrow, curled brim. _ The recent rise in the price of cheese has served to direct public attention to the general increase that has taken place in the cost of living, to the working-classes especially, during the past year. Not only is cheese now selling in the shops at Bd. to Od. per lb., but bacons and hams have also rcached the highest prices known for many years. The food situation thus created is of serious importance to the middle and working classes. Interviewed on the subject, one of the largest pro- vision importers in Tooley-street stated that the advance in prices was ' undoubtedly largely due to the fact that the public were not con- suming as many tinned meats as formerly, as the result of the prejudice which has not yet entirely disappeared against American Tinned Meats, "Corned Beef," he stated, "was a most economic and nourishing food, costing only Gd. per lb.,and there were many signs that the consumption of it was rapidly returning to its old standard." In further conversation, he called attention as a reason why the public should be no longer prejudiced against tinned meats to a public statement made the other day by the Hon. G. P. Cable, Solicitor for the United States Department of Agriculture, in which he stated that he was authorised to say that the American Government had made, and would continue to make, the United States stamp upon meat food products, stand for the highest standard of excellence.

aacr snrpi.aco a new emotion on his face; the feeling of one who sees his hope realised. Her thoughts feasted on his look. How glad, how 3ontent he seemed! surely he had his heart's desire. She was in a tremour of happiness. "I am glad, glad," she said. it is good to have seen his ,face like that. It is worth all the past, the years of waiting, the silence, the doubt, to see his face now. And his heart's desire is my heart's desire." _ _

A great silence seemed to hold her thought suspended-. It made a breathless hush about her. In that pause of life and emotion th, vicar's 'mice iiajled forth like a slow, deep knell. "I publish the banns of marriag, vetween lohn Lawson, bachelor, of this parish; and Elizabeth Barnes, widow, of the parish of St. Marylebone, London." - One by one the words dropped in the charged silence of the startled congregation deep, deep down to the consciousness of woman who had heard only the two names, John Lawson, Elizabeth Barnes. _ _ _

She sat without breath, motionlesS, making no sign of feeling. The knell seemed to be across a sea of silence, a sea that yct had voic?, and surged about her in other words: "Thy heart's desire. Thy heart's desire." She knelt down with the rest of the congrega- tion, and the words of the prayers rattled about her ears like earth on a coffin lid. By force of long habit, she put out her hand at the third collect, and felt for the music of the anthem. Yes, it was there. In another minute she would have to get up and sing the solo. "He shall give thee thy heart's desire."

The words boat about her humbled head. She put up her hands as if to ward off the blows: "Ah, no! No I can't sing that—l can't sing that —I can't sing that now." And yet she opened her eyes and rose with the congregation, and by force of long habit, took up the music and opened it. She bent her eyes on the page, but there were no notes. All was dark. It 'did not seem strange to her. The great dark- ness that had blotted out hope would also hide the music from her. It was quite natural that night should have fallen. No, it did not seem strange at first. She passed her hand across her eyes and a little wondering sigh escaped her. Then thought flashed out, and she shuddered from head to foot. What was this? What?

The minute after, a swift, glad relief thrilled through her, and she almost cried aloud. It was nothing. Mary Leake had once told her that she could not see after rising from prayer. Seraphina drew a long breath as she remembered the glare of sunlight in the church, the light in which she had seen John Lawson's face glorified. She shaded her eyes and bent her gaze on the music she held. And a horror of great darkness fell upon her. "I am blind. lam blind."

She did not strive or cry, sitting rigid, while the organ pealed out the bars of the music she was to eine. _ _ _

"Ile shall give thee thy heart's desire." She fought with the knot in her throat, gulping down her revolt. "I can't sing it. I can't sing it now."

She was groping among her thoughts straining to get away from the organ, from that intolerable "I am blind, blind ; and she was not conscious of the silence in the church that waited for her.

Then Mary Leake plucked at her skirt, whisper- ing: "Sing, miss, sing, or they will know as your' re upset." Seraphina rose vaguely, slowly drawing herself to her feet. .

"yes, she must sing, or all Brook would know that the blow had crushed her. She must sin.. ot John Lawson would know that the shock of disappointment had made her blind." One hand gripping the shelf in front steadied her; the other still held the score. She felt Mary Leake's knee pressed against her shaking limbs. She could not see the faces bent on he curious, pitying, peering to see how she had 'borne the announcement that had staggered them. Her own eyes were lifted to the deep shadow where there was no pity. Clear, and strong the sweet words came: "Oh, rest in the Lord, wait patiently for Him. Trust also in Him, and He shall give thee thy heart's desire. And He shall give thee thy heart's desire."

At the repetition the words swayed uncertainly, and Mary Leake did not breathe, but pressed closet against her mistress. The voice steadied again, and went on to the final bars, full and strong and ringing. . _ _ . . - _

"Oh, rest in the Lord.- Oh, reai the Lord." Once more a charged silence held all the throbbing hearts in the church, a silence that broke in sighs about the rustle that told that Seraphina had taken her seat again. Mary Leake turned her streaming eyes to her mistress, and sobbed aloud. The youth had gone out with the light from Seraphina's face. It was grey, and old, and stricken.

John Lawson lifted his grave eyes in the sun- light. ' "it is true," he said to himself. "He siill give thee thy heart's desire."

eignor Brune, the Italian inventor whose name is associated with wireless telephones, has now perfected another great departure in swift ,electric communication. His new invention is one for sending telephonic and telegraphic mes- sages simultaneously along the same wire, by means of an arrangement setting in motion electrio waves of varying energy which neither overlap nor mingle. The control is so complete that messages can be exchanged between any two points, notwithstanding that other stations intervene. In some experiments excellent results were obtained from Copparo to Ferrara,, Bo- logna, and Florence.

She could bear her loss with • fortitude since it had not changed his feeling towards her. Indeed, his manner had a deeper kindliness, a more protective right than before. He took the oversight of her life into his hands, and controlled and mapped out her work so as to lessen the strain 'on her sight. And Seraphina accepted his silent devotion with passionate gratitude. • His continued silence 'did not trouble her. He Find always been reserved, and his wooing would be characteristic. Probably one Sunday he would wait, for her when church was Over, and they would walk home together, and he would startle her with an abrupt: "Well, my little friend, when shall we be married ? " He had never said a word of love to her, but where two people understood each other words were unnecessary. She had never breathed her love for him, but he knew she loved him. He must know. She hung her head, blushing. Did not all Brook know her secret? Since last summer the love had grown and developed. His care had changed her life. It had changed him too. He was more human, less remote ; and she was not too blind to see the happiness that brooded in his eyes. It had not been there when she went to London ; but since her return it seemed to be bringing' back his youth. And she was growing young too. Fate had been very kind to spare her disfigurement.. ENGL:SHWOMEN IN ITALY.

A large number of Englishwomen earn their living in Italy, of Whom. nearly all zre either governesses or nurses, writes a correspondent. "No nurse is really to be trusted with one's chit- as an Italian once said to me, "but the l..ast untrustworthy form of nurse is the English one." The Royal nurseries have always been presided over by Englishwomen, and it is quite the exception for children of the upper classes not to have an English nurse or governess from their earliest years. This accounts for the fluent English that one hears in Italian society, and indeed many. 'Romans seem to speak English constantly even amongst themselves. As a nurse is expected to be always with her little charges, she is not obliged to associate with the servants, so the position is very suitable for a lady; and though Italians are not yet accustomed to the idea of a woman of good birth undertaking such work, yet if the Englishwoman has tact and adaptability, and is unmistakably a •lady, she is treated with great kindness, and since Italian mothers like to take their children about with them everywhere, a lady nurse Often has a very pleasant and interesting life, though the salary given is not usually a large one.

SPOTTED SILK BLOUSE

Pictured below we have a dainty silk blouse— white spotted with red. Spots are as fashionable as ever, and when the trimmings match or con- trast well have a cachet all their own. The round yoke and collar-band are of coarse lace, and a minute butterfly bow finishes the neck in front. The trimming consists of red velvet

bands, which must be most correctly shaped to fit closely to the figure; and the panel slips into a waistband of the same red velvet. The sleeves are of elbow length, slightly puffed, and finished with a narrow band of velvet. A brunette would look well in a blouse of this description; though it is surprising how well the colour be comes certain types of blonde, particularly the shade known as " soldier-red."

MELBA-THE SOCIAL QUEEN

It is an interesting fact that a famous singer who gives amply all that her great artistic posi- tion exacts should have found the time and diS- position to become a social leader in the great world. - In this respect, says M. A. P., Melba is unique, for she. is the only prima-donna of the time who, apart from the privileges of her art. fills a prominent place in the beau-monde. A pronounced contempt for all shams, pride born of a great innate dignity, or the heritage of a laudable family prejudice—or perhaps all three combined—have throughout her career made Madame Melba decline to allow herself to be used as a figure in the recognised parades of the, fakionable world merely because of her emi- nence as a singer. When she goes into society she goes not as a privileged professional, but as a member of society, and to those whose hospi- tality she accepts she comes as an equal, ad re- turns hospitality as correct, as lavish, and asi stately as that which she receives. But con- spicuous success in society has not lessened Melba's interest in her humbler friends, and the struggling artist or social derelict having any claim on her consideration is as sure of a kindly welcome. as the most exalted of Mn. intimates.

THE IDEAL tIOSTEc.;3.

A question I am frequently asked, writes "Lady Evelyn" in the Reader. is, What quali- ties go to make the Ideal Hostess? I think they may be erystallied into the following qualifica- tions: She must make you feel individually that you are the favoured „ guest. She must make you feel perfectly at "home. She must eee every- thing and yet possess the art of seeming to see nothing., She must never look bored. She must know 170 W to get congenial people together. She must know how to keep «mversation always going. She must never let -anyone be slighted or overlooked. She must know when to ask tie amateur musician to display his or her talents. She must be unselfish about her own Pleasures. She must remember that nothing is so tiresome, so surely death to all enjoyment, as the feeling that one is being entertained.'

A BLIND LOVE.

BY ANNIE E. HOLDSWORTH. Ikuthor of "Joanna Trail', Spinster," "The Yeas that the Locust Hath Eaten," &c., &c.

The cottage stood like a honeysuckle bower among the roses; and in the front parlour Seraphina was practising the anthem for next Sunday. Up and down her voice went, clear, still and sweet, but not so strong as it had been when she first came to Brook, and Dr. Lawson had gone to church to hear the pretty little schoolmistress sing.

I She was like her voice, sweet and true and dainty but the strength had gone out of her colouring.. Hair and complexion had faded, and her eyes were not so bright as they had been. She ' shaded them from the glare when she glanced out of the window along the road that threaded the scattered houses of 'the village. She could see as far as the moss-covered bridge that crossed the brook.

--When Dr. Lawson came over the bridge, and the 'notes shook with her quickened breath she would have time to steady her voice before he slackened pace at the sweetbriar hedge and came in to tea— for it was Friday. On other days Seraphina smiled at him from the window, her eyes brightening, her face reddening in its honeysuckle frame. Sometimes she happened to be in the garden when he passed, and then she would give him a bit Of lavender., or a spcig of Lad's-love for his buttonhole.

Lately she had begun to see an ungentle humour in offering him the Lad's-love. Time had not i stood still with the doctor, and the ten years that had gathered her roses had changed him into a crusty bachelor. Nobody in Brook knew the secret of John Lawson's loneliness and reserve. There were some whom his lonelginess offended; for in spite of his gravity he had a kind heart, and Seraphina, with her sunny temper, was the very wife for him. At first things had been hopeful. He had turned his glasses towards her when she sang in church, and had not moved his eyes till she had finished. He had been seen On summer evenings strolling towards the cottage, and pausing at the gate to beg a buttonhole. It was known that he sometimes took tea with her,, and that he spent nearly every Friday evening in the cottage. She gave him lessons in German, and he corrected the children's exercises for her. But all this had gone on for ten years, and old Mrs. Leake complained that "the courting got no forrader," and Brook fostered a grudge against the doctor. Brook knew that the little schoolmistress shared their hopes. She never looked so pretty as when . she stood with lifted eyes leading the sopranos while John Lawson's hoarse notes charged head- long through the bass. If the doctor's seat was empty the sopranos had no spirit in them, and Mary Leake would look her sympathy at her beloved mistress. Mary knew just how much her mistress thought of the doctor. She knew for whom the best tea was brewed, and the most delicate scones baked, and the finest jam brought out. The day that Seraphina was ruthless with her William Allen Richardson roses, Mary put on her whitest apron and cap and bustled happily among the tea-cups. The curate did not know a wild rose from a sweetbriar, and her second best apron was good enough for him. But she did wish the doctor's courting was not so slow. She wished she could give him a hint to hasten his steps, for though Miss Glynn was as sweet and happy as ever, she was not growing younger. Mary, who sang in the choir and had a critical ear, had sometimes noticed the quaver in the high notes; and more than once lately Miss Glynn had rung for another candle, though the reading lamp burnt steadily beside her. But, then the doctor, who was not an old man, openly put on his glasses to listen to the anthem at morning service. Seraphina had sung the solo in the anthems everD Sunday for ten years, and all Brook would have liken staggered if she ha ol chanced to omit it.. She was practising for Sunday now, walking up and down in the parlour singing her solo: "And He shall give thee thy heart's desire. And He shall give thee thy heart's desire." She repeated the words with a dainty precision, but her voice failed at the concluding bars, "Oh, rest in the Lord." A minute after she appeared in the kitchen door- way, the score in her hand, her face troubled. "Mary, look at. this music. Can you. see the notes? Do they run together and look blurred?" "No, ma'am; they are just the same as ever." Mary glanced from the page to her mistress. "Curious," Seraphina said thoughtfully, "now I see them too. Yet a minute ago I could not see that line. There it is quite plain . . . . Oh, of course, how foolish I am!" She laughed consciously, and avoided Mary's gaze. "I was looking out of the window, and the glare, dazzled me. Then when I turned to the music I could not see clearly." "Yes, ma'am, that was it," Mary answered soberly. "Often in church when I opens my eyes after the prayers I sees nothing for a minute or two till I gets accustomed to the light." "Of course." Seraphina laughed again, and tripped gaily back to the window to watch for Dr. Lawson.

He came up the path flicking the dust from his I boot with his handkerchief. He knocked, opened the door, and went into the parlour. "Tea ready ? " he sang out in a voice as big as himself. "How de do, Miss Glynn? Evening, Mary; how good your scones smell." Seraphina was used to his manner. It did not quite accord with the pressure he gave her hand. She allowed him to retain it while he searched her face with his keen eyes. "H'in! You don't look quite so washed-out as you did last week. Feeling better, eh ? "-. Seraphina's happy eyes were downcast. She blushed faintly when she drew away and took her at at table. But she lost her self-consciousness on seeing his enjoyment of the jam and scones. He ate like n schoolboy, and she thought him delightful. He did not say much, but she was quite content to feast her eyes on him. With him opposite to her she never remembered the slow passage of those ten years. After tea he got up in a business-like manner, and ..aked for the exercise-books. They sat down -.-oberly together, and began, correcting the pages. All at once Seraphina started. "There it is again. How strange!" The doctor let his pince-nez drop and stared at her. "What is strange ? " be asked. "A thought I had. Doctor, do people ever lose their sight suddenly ? " "Well, yes—though it's tare. In certain affec- tions of the optic nerve blindness may occur as the result of shock. And there is nervous blindness. But what makes you ask? If you are thinking of Mrs. Leake's cataract, that is quite another thin..." "I wasn't thinking of her. To-day a cloud has oome over my eyes twice, and I wondered—" "Nonsense! Your sight is excellent. Let me

He turned her face to the light and peered into her eyes. "You have no difficulty in seeing, have 3rou ? Any spots before your eyes? Can you read from this distance? " _ _

4.4 lY••••••., • He held up a folded newspaper with the column of "Births, marriages, and deaths" before her. "I can see the names in small capitals," she said. "The first name is Barnes, but I can't see if it's a man or a woman. Wait ; I can make out the next: At 14, High-street, Marylebone ' ; but I can't go any further." She looked anxiously at the doctor. His eyes vere remote; he seemed to have forgotten her existence. There was a long, curious pause, then he roused himself. She felt rather .than heard the alteration in his voice. He was forcing himself to remember what she had been saying. "Barnes, you said ; but you don't know if it is a man or a woman. Well, you may see better with spectacles. Come to the surgery to-morrow, and I will examine the sight properly." Seraphina laid down the paper and laughed. "I hope you are not going to Order glasses. I don't want to be an old woman vet." . _

WYCLIFFE CHURCH YEAR BOOK.

The year book of the Wycliffe Congregational Church with the reports for DOG has just been issued and con- tains a mass of useful information as to the progress of the church- and its numerous organizations. The respected pastor (Rev. John Yonge) .ddresses a most interesting letter to the congregation. "Itis " he says "thirty years since I wrote my first pastoral note as editor of the ' Wycliffe Year Book.' ,Between 1876 and 1906 there stretches a long period,having in many respects a chequered history,and yet,despite all its many and con- tinual changes, it has been a period characterized by innumerable blessings and not a few precious successes. 'Having obtained help of God, we continue unto this day.' We are now suffering, as all mid-town churches are suffering everywhere, from the emigration of families to the suburbs. Our great encouragement, however, is that many of our suburban friends are among the most conspicuously regular in their church attendance. If our people who live in town were as little affected by bad weather as are our people at Stockton Heath, Latchford, and Padgate-lane we should have far larger congregations than are customary when rain or cold prevail. It is very poor comfort to learn that our experience is no worse than that of other places of worship. The steadily spreading indifference among even religious people to the House of God is a feature of present day life which is full of very sinister omen for the Christian churches, and equally so for the Chris- tian life of our population. It is a fact which calls for the renewed devotion and conscientious loyalty of members of all the churches of Jesus Christ, and I trust it will not cal,l in vain upon our Wycliffe people. In his notes upon finance our Treasurer has dwelt appreciatingly upon the admirable response riled@ by nearly all our community to the recent appeal for church renovation. Both the external and internal appearance of our church have been rendered attractive and worthy of our hest Wycliffe traditions. SOUTH LANCASHIRES AT CHURCH. Special Musical Service.

A service which will rank as a memorable one in the history of the Warrington Parish Church was held on Sunday morning. The men of the 2ncl South Lancashire Regiment who came from the South of England to take part in the ceremonies attendant upon the unveiling of the Soldiers' Memorial were present. and their fine band and drums accompanied the service throughout. There was also a large muster of the local Volunteers accom- panied by the band of the battalion. The congregation was one of the largest ever seen at the Church, every seat being occupied while a great number •of people stood in the aisles and on the gallery steps. The service was highly impressive, tae Danl and choir being heard with beautiful effect. As an open- ing voluntary the band played Handel's " Largo " and afterwards the hymn " Forty days and 'forty nights" was sung. Ttie band accompanied all the hymns, psalms and canticles and the roll of the drums, particularly in "Onward Christian soldiers," added much to the impressiveness of the music. The Te Deum was sun 5 to Sir R. Stewarts' setting and as an anthem the choir interpreted" I will give thanks"—to the music of the Gloria from Mozert's 12th Mass—in a Manner which reflected great credit upon them. Mr. F. M. Darby, AI us. Bac. presided at the organ. The congre- gation included the Mayor and Mayoress and Miss Smethurst. The sermon was preached by the Rector from the words," And the king said: What shall be - done unto the man whom the King delighteth to honour?' Then said the Xing, 'Thus shall it be done unto the lean whom the King delighteth to honour.' Towards the close of the discourse the Rector alluded to the late Colonel O'Leary whom he - said, they all held in honour and veneration. The service concluded with the stirring " Tanhauser March," and afterwards the buglers sound "The Last Post "in the churchyard. A procession was then formed headed by the regimental and bugle bands of the Volun- teer regiment, among the officers present being Colonel Edelsten commanding. Tne band and men of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment followed. At Markct Gate the procession was divided, the Volunteers proceeding direct to the Barracks while the 2nd South Lancashire contingent marched alone Bridge-street, round Arpley Cannons, Wilson Fatten-street, and Parker-street,to the Barracks. Large crowds lined the route and owing to the confusion caused by the division of the procession at Market Gate there were two accidents. A number of young men rushed wildly into Bridge-street and several ladies had narrow escapes from being knocked down. One youth, about 22 years of age, was knocked down, and when it was seen that he had been injured, was picked up by the police and carried to the steps of rinity Church out of further danger. It was found that one of his legs bad been injured, and he was placed in a cab, driven home, and atten ed by a medical Man. A man was knoo ed down in Bridge-street and trampled upon and his face and shoulders -injured. He fainted, and it was some time before he was able to proceed home.

DOES 'IRON GROW?

Tile technical journals of the world have been seussing the question, "Can Iron Grow ? " rithe Iron Age, commenting on certain experi- Inents, states that all experiments show a re- Markable difference between cast-iron on the '°rle hand and steel or wrought-iron on the other, -when heated red-hot, allowed to coal, and re heated a number of times. It was shown that ,onless cast-iron was heated to a cherry red it (loes not permanently expand. This was proved by means of bars heated daily for nine hours for more than a year in an oven and cooled over- night. There was no permanent change. The increase in length of rails on a swing-bridge may be attributable to a purely mechanical cause, namely, the stretching of the rails, duo to the rolling of heavy trains across them. Heating and cooling day and night would not -apprect- r-hly change the length of rails permanently. 'This, of course, has nothing to do with the tem- porary change in length, duo to changes of tern- iperat ure.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ORIGIN OF DREAMS,

-The fullest examination into the nature and :origin of dreams has been made by M. Moreau, the French scientist. He divided dreams into the dreams of health and the dreams that are the result of a diseased state of mind or body. With regard to the latter, it need only be remarked, that persistent dreaming is one of the most in- variable symptoms of insanity, and as it is a psychological fact that genius is near allied to madness men of genius must be expected to ilream more than ordinary men. With regard to the dreams of health, they are the result of an imperfect state of sleep, according as we look At it. It is obvious that the mind of the clever itan that is constantly oc.:.upied with many .schemes and thoughts is more liable to be awake when. his body is asleep, than that of the dullard, 'whose mind is often asleep when his 'body is •awake. Thus the sleep of the plough. boy has become proverbial on account of its un- sdisturbed nature. In most cases P. man of lively imagination and quick brain is undoubtedly more liable to dream than the dullard.

VOLCANIC FERTILISERS.

That there is a silver lining to every cloud, Dr. Stoklasa, professor at the Technical High School at Prague, again proves in the results of his tests in connection with the eruption of Mount Vesuvius. In fact, according to his cal- culations, the crater threw out upward of fifty _milliard kilos of volcanic mud, sand, lava, ashes, 40.. upon the surrounding ground. Those-, he ;estimates, contain an average of at least o'l per ,cent, nitrogen in the form of ammonia. The 3inountain has consequently produced about fifty ;million kilos of fertiliser, representing more :ammonia and nitrous acid than is used in the .whole •of England. He has found besides enor- mous deposits of potassium phosphate and other -fertilisers readily assimilated by the vegetable •kingdom. ' The vapours constantly rising from the mouth of Vesuvius already contain much plant food, in fact the surroundings of volcanoes are altvays highly fertile, and have no need whatever of artificial fertilisers. Dr. Stoklasa has shewn that ammonia is always rising from 3the crater Its white smoke.

A CURIOSITY IN ENCYSTMENT. Pr 00069 . known ais encyfftment QT m 4144-

tam is common iimong the lowest animals. Tx', protozoa. In this transformation they become motionless and surround themselves with a thick coating, or cyst. In the Zoologist Mr. James Murray describes a curious encystment under- gone by those curious creatures known as "water-bears." These animals are of a much higher grade than the protozoa, being allied to the mites and ticks. This encystment took the for/in of little sausage-shaped packages. When these were squeezed -water-bears in a motionless state emerged. This was shewn to be an early stage in ,the encystment. The cyst was double, the outer one with six rudimentary legs and the ,iner without these appendages. Afterwards it shrinks to an almost amorphous mass something like a worm in appearance. Pxofessor Lauter- born has shown that a similar curious encyst- ment takes place in the case of a Continental species of water-bear. What is the object or use of this strange transformation, as well as its further history, have still to be made out.

Almost everybody knows the rule of the masons that stone used in building shoukl be so placed that it will lie as it lay in its natural bed when quarried. But Mr. Francis W. Hoyt, in the Engineering News, says that this familiar rule is not always to be depended upon, and needs in many cases to be supplemented with other precautions. There are three planes of fracture known to quarrymen. The "rift" is the direction in which the stone splits most easily; the "grain" that which is next easiest; the " head" that which offers the greatest re sistance. In a paving-block the two sides; reptle sent the rift fracture, the top and bottom the grain, and the ends the head. But in a quarry the natural bed is sometimes considerably is.- dined to the plane of the rift; hence the ii perfection of the ordinary rule for placing tio' stone in building.

HOW IT FEELS TO DIE.

The Hindu Spiritual Magazine has given several narratives of the experiences of those who have almost died and who were afterwards restored to life.' Of these the most remarkable is the case of Dr. Wiltse, of the St. Louis Medi- cal and Surgical Journal, who, according to his own account, died of typhus fever—at least so nearly died that the church • bell was tolled for his death. He lay pulseless and apparently life- less for half-an-hour. Needles were thrust into his legs without producing any effect. While the doctor lay motionless, apparently dead, his soul, he tells us. was never more intensely alive: "I realised my condition, and reasoned calmly thus: I have died as men term death, and yet I am as much a man as over. I am about to get out of the body. I watched the interesting pro- cess of the separation of soul and body.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
How THE SOUL ESCAPES FROM THE BODY.

"By some power, apparently not my own, the Ego was rocked to and fro, literally as a cradle is rocked, by which process its connection with The tissues of the body was broken up. After a little time the lateral motion oeased, and along the soles of the feet, beginning at the toes, pass- ing rapidly to the heels, I felt and heard, as it seemed, the snapping of innumerable small cords. When this was accomplished. I began slowly to retreat from the feet toward the head as a rubber cord shortens. I remember reaching the hips and saying to myself, 'Now there is no life below the hips.' I can recall no memory of passing through the abdomen and chest, but recollect distinctly when my whole self was col- lected into the head, when I reflected thus: I am all in the head now, and I shall soon be free. I passed around the brain as if I were' hollow, compressing it and its membranes slightly on ali sides toward the centre, and peeped out between the sutures of the skull, emerging like the flat- tened edges of a bag of membranes. I recollect distinctly how I appeared to myself something like a jelly-fish as regards colour and form.. THE FIRST SENSATIONS OP FREEDOM.

"As I emerged from the head I floated up and down and laterally like a soap-bubble attached to the bowl of a pipe, until I at last broke loose from the body and fell lightly' to the floor, where I slowly rose and expanded into the full stature of a man. I seemed to be trans- lucent, of a bluish cast, and- perfectly naked. With a painful sense of embarrassment I fled toward the partially open door to escape the eyes of the two ladies whom I was facing OS well as others whom I knew were about me, but upon "aching the door I found myself clothed, and ...tisfled upon that point I turned and faced the company.' _

saw the mourners round his corpse, and tried ..in vain to make them realise his presence. He marvelled that he felt so wonderfully well; he passed out of doors and travelled swiftly through the air. But he did not pass into the other world.

Three prodigious rocks blocked his path. His hour had not yet come: He became unconscious again, and when he was lying in his bed he awoke to consciousness and soon recovered. He wrote out this narrative eight weeks after his strange experience, but he told it to those at the bedside as sOon as he re- vi-ed. The doctor, who was at the bedside, sa..l that the breath was absolutely extinct so tar as coma cc ouservea, aria every symptom marking tho patient as dead was present. NEW ELECTRIC FURNACE.

In order to determine the points of fusion a refractory substances, W. C. The so-called "ginger-beer plant," which, placed in a suitable solution produces ginger. beer by fermentation, is formed by the grow- ing together of two plants, a yeast- and a b,cillus. They are associated in what biolo- gists term symbiosis. Each organism in a ease of this kind contributes something to the general good, and receives something from the other. In the Bulletin of the Mycological Society of France M. L. Lutz gives an account of a similar symbiotic association of a yeast and a fungus. It is known as " Tibi," and is used in i,lexico along with sugar to form a fermented liquor. M. Lutz has experimented with tibi with the, object of ascertaining if the yeast could be in- duced to grow in symbiosis with any other bacillus than that with which it is normally asso- ciated. Various bacilli were tried, but success- ful growths were only obtained with one, viz. bacillus subtilis. The two organisms formed small creamy balls which produced the fermenta- tion. In every other ease one of the organisms gradually displaced the other so that finally' only a yeast or a bacillus remained. The function of the bacillus in this plant community is said tr be to form an anwrobic environment, that is one free from oxygen, for the yeast plant.

SPONTANEOUS IGNITION OF COAL. The old hypothesis suggested by Liebig, att cording to which the spontaneous inflammitti- of coal is due to • the oxidation of the pyrites, can no longer be maintained. Spontaneous ignition, as pointed out by Dr. lieideprim in a German technical magazine, would seem rather to be attributable to a direct oxidation of the carbon. In fact, carbon when heated. has been Sound eagerly to absorb oxygen from the air, and this heating effect can be increased until ignition occurs. The part played by moisture in the proces has not yet been determined. The physical conditions of the carbon (hardness, size. Sic.) are other factors influencing the pro- cess. In connection with a recent investigation of WO cases of self-ignitien, all kinds of mineral coal apart from anthracite Were examined.' In nio4 cases the ignited coal was ordinary coal, and less frequency nut coal or coal dust. The higher the layers, the inure readily will self. ignitu take place. An efficient ventilation by channels in the coal layers and the thermometri- cal recording of temperatures by long thermo- meters inserted in the coal have been found to be good preventive mea.sares. while the only available means of exting,uiehing such fires has been found to be a transfer of. the coal. and Simultaneous_ flooding.

SMALLEST STEAM ENGINE ON EARTE". What is perhaps the smallest stationary engine ever constructed has been completed at his shop in. Yonge - street by Thomas H. Robinson, watchmaker, of Toronto, Ontario. Smaller than a conpuon housefly, it slip easily, says thA Scientifir _American, into a 22 short" empty cartridge with plenty of room to spare. The vibrating piston rod when running at this speed emits a sound like that produced by a mosquito. The bore of the cylinder is 3-100th of an inch; the strokp is 1-32 of an inch. The cylinder and lifiton rod, shaft, and crank are of steel. The engine bed and stand are of gold. THE CAUSE OF SLEEP.

Sir William Gower's, the famous medical scien- tist, has developed a new theory of sleep. According to his explanation the suspension of consciousness in sleep is probably due to a "break and make" action among the brain cells. The activity of the brain is considered to be due to nerve cells, from which spring nerve cords that go on dividing and sub-dividing, until they terminate in little knobs. Formerly it was believed that the nerve cells of the brain were in permanent connection by means of thei# terminals; but now it appears that these are only in opposition, and capable of being sepo- rated. The hypothesis is that during sleep separation takes place, and the fact that nar- cotic substances are capable of inducing slosp is held to support this view.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
"ELECTRIC STEEL" IN GERMANY

The Heronit electric process for the produc- tion of steel is now in practical operation at Remscheid Haston in Germany, and it is re- ported that the steel produced is much superior in many ways to that made by older methods. Steel of great purity and homogeneous quality is made from ordinary scrap-iron rubbish, melted, and then subjected to the action of an electric oven, in which the necessary carbon, manganese, nickel and other substances required to produce steel of various qualities are added to the liquid metal. The oven has a capacity of from one and one-half to two tons, and is heated by a current of 100 volts. The new steel is said to be stronger and more resistant to wear and tear than the old-fashioned crucible steel.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
MEASURING THE INFINITE.

Of all the sciences, astronomy, probably, is the most impressive and awe-inspiring. The space within the confines of the solar system of which earth is part, and the outermost known member of which is nearly 3,000,000,000 miles from the centre, is but a drop in the ocean of space. We think of and measure distances on earth in term: of an inch or a yard or a mile. The smallor. yard-stick, so to speak, with which the sem._ nomer measures distance in the universe is the semi-diameter of the earth's orbit, roughly speaking, 93,000,000 miles. Such figures are of very little use to the ordinary person, but they may serve, says the _Pictorial Magaeine, to give some notion of the grandeur of that human intel- lect which can unravel and systematise the mys- teries of the stars.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ORIGIN OF GUNPOWDER,

The Chinese have long been credited with the invention of gunpowder, but Professor E. 0. von Lipprnaan, of Halle, has collected evidence to indicate that this is a mistake, and that the Arabians did not, as commonly stated, introduce gunpowder into Europe during the eighth and ninth centuries. Professor von Lippmann be. 'levee that the manufacture of the first gun- powder was based upon the " Fire-book ' of Marcus Grimm, which appeared in Constanti- nople about the middle of the thirteenth cen- tury. This was the source from which Roger Bacon, Albertus Magnus, and Thomas Aquinas derived their knowledge of gunpowder. The first use of gunpowder to drive projectiles is ascribed to a monk, Berthold Schwarz, whose discovery was made accidentally while preparing the mix- ture for medicinal purposes.

THE TRAINED COOK.

The Royal Princesses, King Edward's sisters were, as children, taught to cook, and many of our noble ladies have enjoyed a similar training. Clever cooks, be they gentle or simple, are invariably liberal in tt eir use of currants, knowing the wonderful nutritive properties of this fruit. The simplest way in which currants can be eaten is in bread. Currant bread, when thinly cut, is a really excellent food, being most nutritious and easily digested.

"And the neighbours—are they quiet and respectable ? " "Yes, indeed, sir," the young man replied. "I can't speak for the people at 67 personally, but the tenant is a Colonel Gosford, so he ought to be all right. On the other side—in the house that fronts to Wimborne-street—a widow lady resides with her daughter. Charming people. We were standing in the drawing-room, where we had duly admired the Grecian dado, and with businesslike alacrity the clerk prepared to accoin- pany us by drawing down the blinds which we had raised for the purpose of our inspection. This he had done in all the rooms as we left them in order to keep the sun from fading the expensive carpets. So we quitted the house and crossed to the opposite side of the street, where., on reaching the kerb, Laura turned round, with a woman's interest in her future abode, to throw a parting glance at the house. As she did so an exclamation escaped her.

"See, oh see!" she cried. "Ah, he's gone, but I saw his finger on the blind. lie has just drawn it aside to peep at us." I turned to the agent's clerk, who was trying to conceal an amused smile. We had been into every room in the house from cellar to attics, and there had been no traces of occupation, but I put the question, "Thus is a caretaker there, I suppose?" "Oh, dear no, sir," the young man replied. "I am positive that there is no one in the house. The lady was probably deceived by the shadow of that lamp-post falling on the blind." "That must have been it," I assented, for I had looked up quickly and had seen nothing. "Come, dear, let us go and finish the business and get back to lunch."

With one final scrutiny of the veiled window Laura turned away, but before we reached the agent's office she had contrived to whisper so that our guide should not hear: "I am certain that I was not mistaken. Do let us consider a little longer before deciding." We had been married six months and I had never known her to be nervous before, but this flying in the face of a proved fact could be accounted for in no other way. I laughed at her, and passed it off by remarking that if there was a man in the house now he had better cgar out before we moved in.

again." - Ever since my visit to Blaikie in the morning the house had been getting on my nerves, but the prospect of "joining issue," as we lawyers say, with the mystery came as a bracing tonic. If Laura was wrong about. the wardrobe the chances were that she had been wrong about the finger on the blind. In which case the sham Colonel Gosford was probably an ordinary sneak-thief, and the mental aspersions which I had begun to cast upon our abode were groundless. Treading bravely I walked to the ward- robe, flung open the door, and was instantly seised by the throat. Thus holding me at arm's length in a grasp of iron a man stepped down from the wardrobe and looked me in the eyes. He was a tall fellow, not ill-looking, and wore a light overcoat over evening dress. On his head was an opera hat. "It is Colonel Gosford," whimpered Laura. I had not confided to her my interview with Blaikie. "Remain where you are, madam, and if you value this gentleman's life do not call out," the man said, sternly, for she had begun to scramble out of bed. "Now, sir, you are the tenant of this house, I presume ? " . So far as the pressure of my windpipe would permit I signified consent. My assailant had drawn a revolver, ptoving that resistance was useless. He remained silent for a few minutes, thinking deeply. "You have put me to a good deal of inconvenience by taking this house, still more by unexpectedly occupying this room to-night," he said at length. "For these reasons I cannot see that I owe you any consideration. But there is the lady to be thought of, and for her sake I should wish to be gallant enough to let you down easy. Can you suggest a way out of the difficulty yourself, always remembering that any attempt at raising an alarm will mean your instant death ? " He loosed his hold of me, but kept his pistol handy. "Do anything he asks, Reggie," Laura wailed. "Here, let him take this," and she began to fumble under her pillow, finally producing her watch. The stranger smiled frostily. "Pray do not hint at such a thing, madam," he said. "I am in a much larger way of business than watches. I entered this house as a fugitive, not in the practice of my profession." "How can I suggest a way out of the difficulty when I don't know what it is?" I asked, sullenly. "My dear sir, of course not," the intruder replied, soothingly. "I will put the problem before you as briefly as possible. I am, perhaps, the most extensive producer of spurious bank paper in Europe. lam badly wanted by the police, who are at this moment searching 24, Wimborne-street for me, on the other side of the wall against which stands that most convenient wardrobe. My object is to escape them just as yours must be to escape this pistol, which I shall assuredly use upon you if you try to stop me. If you see it in the right light ' our interests are really identical." In spite of the clatter curiosity prevailed. "But how did you get into my house?" I stammered. "Come now, that question augurs well for a mutual agreement," the man smiled. "I got in, Mr. Conway, through the hinged back of the wardrobe, and through a false section of the party wall behind it—both of which were contrived for the purpose of affording me a bolt-hole when my mother and my wife took 24, Wimborne-street. I do not work there, and to-night's adventure has rendered the arrangement useless for the future, so Ido not mind telling you. I have only once before had to use it since you have been in residence—on the day when Mrs. Conway surprised, me coming downstairs and I gave the name of your neighbour on the other side." "And a dog followed you through, and got shut in this room ? " "Exactly, and my wife came round and hoaxed you that it had run in through your area door," the stranger said, grinning. 'Now, my dear Mr. Conway, you will, lam sure, allow me to follow my usual line of retreat—down your stairs and out of your front door. Our paths are not likely to cross again as my people will have to move after this, and I shouldn't be surprised to hear that you had taken a dislike to this house." There was a touch of irony in this request, seeing that his finger toyed with the trigger of the revolver, and that underneath his smooth polite- ness lay the recninti.... .f t1,.01v nurntwe Tiara.

An interesting event took place on Thursday evesisse last week at Penketh House the residence of Mr. James Dodds, in the form of a presentation to Mr. David Houghton, P.G.M., of the Order of Druids. Mr. George Maybury (Order of Foresters) in a few el 1 chosen words, referring to the advancement and good work of Friendly Societies, called on Mr. Dodds to preside over the gathering, which was veay representative. _ _

'Mr. Dodds in opening the proceedings spoke in very feeling and sympathetic terms of Mr. Houghton in his connection with Friendly Society work, and especially in giving his best services for the benefit of others. He hoped that these men would see that his actions in the past had been dictated for their good and for the upbuilding and security of their Order. He concluded by calling on Alderman William Pierpoint to present Mrs. Houghton with a beautifully framed enlargement of a photograph Mr. Houghton, by Mr, Birtles. I In doing so Alderman Pierpoint alluded to the long I term of years during which he had known Mr. Houghton —the knowledge of a life of strenuous service and lofty motive. He admired a man who strove for honesty of purpose and purity and fidelity in the discharge of public trusts. He congratulated him on his recent succest in the Law Courts, and hoped it would bring some satisfaction and compensation for all the trouble and annoyance he had gone through. Mr. Aitken at the call of the Chairman, next presented Mr. Houghton with a purse of gold. Subscribed for by many of his fallow townsmen and admirers throughout the country. He spoke in every eulogistic terms of his knowledge of and contact with the recipient in his daily life. He admired his ideals of fidelity and devotion to duty, and paid a high compliment to his singleness of purpose. Mr. Houghton with evident emotion, tendered his his heartfelt thanks for the gifts presented, and for the high ccmpliments paid to him by the different speakers and referred with much pride to the greatadvances made by his Order during the term of his office as Grand Master of the Order of Druids. On the motion of Councillor 71ord seconded by Dr. Murray a most hearty and enthusiastic vote of thanks was accorded the host and hostess (Mr. and Mrs. Dodds) and to the Misses Dodds for their genial hospitality and help in the proceedings. A most enjoyable social con- cluded the proceedings.

A steam engine iu electric works at Campine (Ronmania) exploded on Thursday, blowing up the building, and killing two men, A third is dying. Mr. Roosevelt gave a dinner on Thursday night in- honour of retiring Cabinet members. Mr. and Mrs. Bryce were present—their first official function.

THE E XAIVI INER.

MARCH 2nd 1907

Mr. HALDANE on Monday 3,11 t. HALDANE'S expounded in a speech of ARMY SCHEIN' E. more than three hours' dura

tion his scheme for the reorganisation of thl British A.-ray. Since 1901 when Mr. BRODRICK produced his famous Army Corps scheme, the military forces of the Crown have undergone the process of redistribution and re-adjustment three times. The Secretary for War

laid stress upon the fact that this was a soldiers' Budget. He had made at the outset of his adminis- tration of the Army a pact with the distin- guished generals who form the Army Council H 3 has organised the regular Army into six divisions of three brigades each, in fulfilment of the wishes of his military advisers, who are the best judges of the most suitable field unit, and he has given to the infantry divisions those comple- ments of cavalry and artillery of which Manchuria and South Africa alike demonstrated the necessity. So far the SECRETARY FOR WAR has gone with the approval and the acceptance of the country But his heaviest task was to come in the treatment of the auxiliary forces, with which he desigues to provide the necessary force for home defence. It , would be useless to deny that the scheme which. Mr. HALDANE unfolded to the House of Commons will be examined very critically by the country. The military necessity of a force available at home to meet such emergencies as may arise by eeason of raids has been recognised, and the truth of BACON'S dictum that "training of men and arming them are things of defence and no dangerrhas found general admission in the almost universal desire for the spread of the patriotic movement for the enrol- ment in the Volunteer forces. But the problem

===== 0002601_19070302_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
, - - -

with which Mi. lIALDA7ge wns faced wag that o

getting that value out of the auxiliary forces which alone can justify their continuance. For years they have stood —Militia, Yaomany, and Volunteers alike—in a position almost of hostility to the regular Army. They have had no difinite place in the line of defence, they have had none of the organisation or ecnip nent necessary to transform an armed crowd into an effective Army. The auxiliary forces as a whole have, in fact, never been a coherent organisation, with functions to perform and the means to perform them. All that Mr. HALTYANE proposes to alter. The Militia and Volunteers will in future be no disconnected mass vf troops more or less trained. They will form in\ themselves the second line of the Army, a line complete in itself organised on a plan which gives it a definit relation to the first line of a foreign service army. Throughout the county the auxiliary forces will be unified into fourteen big divisions of three brigades each. The Yeomanry will supply the cavalry force necessary to these divisions,- and the Militia and Volunteer artillery will be reconsti- tuted as field artillery, supplied with the fifteen- pounder guns which are being discarded by the regular artillery, and will be converted into quick- firers at a comparatively small cost. Mr. HALDANE has recognised the military truth that no force is of use which is not prepared of itself to undertake service. Without a field organisation the auxiliary forcee were a gigantic fraud, bound to collapse directly they were sub- jected to the test of service. But the provision of a defined function for the Militia and Volunteers is but half the battle. The provision of the men to fill the ranks of this second line army presents perhaps even greater difficulty. Mr. ElltlatANß proposes to surmount it by altering the terms upon which men will enter. In future they will join the Volunteers for four years, and they will be permitted to terminate their engagement on giving three months' notice and paying a floe, which he named approximately at f.. 5. Also they will be liable, if a great mobilisation should be necessary to meet an emergency to go into embodied train- ing for war for six months, by which means they will become efficient to takeetheir places in the fighting line. It will be seen that this is a pro- posal which constitutes a serious depar'aure from the predominating idea of the voluntary- service in the past. Enlistment for four years, with the prospect, if necessity arises to prevent continuance in the ranks, of paying a fine of £5, may not impossibly act as a deterrent with a large class, since the working man cannot be expected to give one month's notice of a change of location,. and 80 • per cent. of the volunteers belong to the working classes.

Warrington is fortunate in WARRINGTON possessing two admirable EISTEDDFOD. male voice choirsr and one of them—the Apollo ehoir —has tor several years undertaken the arrange- ment of an annual Eisteddfod. This is no small venture but the promoters have persevered until the festival is now looked forward to by music- lovers, and is certainly a credit to the town. The last Eisteddfod was exceptionally enjoyable, the arrangements being all that could be desired, while two eminent adjudicators were engaged. The expenses were heavy and we are informed that the aceounts, which have just been completed, show a deficit of £101.5. Toe fact is to be deplored, and it would indeed be a hardship if the burden were to fall upon the choir which works so hard to provide this annual musical treat and, incidentally, is itself debarred from partici- pating in the chief contest. We would suggest that some of our woll-to-do townsmen might show their appreciation of this really valuable effort to encourage musical culture by subscribing the required balance. Warrington Eisteddfod is an institution of which we can all be proud and we hope it may flourish for many years to come.

The Warrington Chamber LOCiL COMIBRCE. of Commerce has completed another year's work and there can be no denying the face that it has repeatedly justified its existence by taking the lead on important matters affecting our commercial life. The chief local subject of discussion during the past twelve months, has been the utilization of the Warrington lay-by and the possible con- struction of a dock at Warrington. We are glad to see that the Chamber is in no way relaxing its efforts to advance these two schemes which would be fraught with such great import to the prosperity of the town. Happily there appears to be a prospect that traders and shipping companies will make much fuller use of the advantages afforded by the lay-by and that Warrington will in time become a regular and important port of call. As far as the dock is concerned, all hope has not yet been aban- doned and we trust the Chamber will use its influence with good results, although it must be confessed that with the expiration of the time allowed for the construction of the dock, the realization of the project is very remote. We congratulated the Chamber on having elected as its president a gentleman of such all-round ability and interest in commercial matters as Mr. _Holman K,ngdon. His report on the work of the Education Committee showed how zealously he has devoted himself to the labours of that authority, and W 3 would call special attention to his plea that employers should concern themselves more fully with the education of their workpeople. In his first speech as President, Mr. Kingdon paid a noteworthy tribute to the present Government, which he considers to be more commercially- inclined than any Government we have ever had. In many ways the Government has undoubtedly given evideac9 of its desire to get into closer touch with commercial life and remove existing 1 anomalies, and Mr. Kingdon's special praise for Mr. Lloyd-George's work at the Board of Trade I will be generally endorsed.

At the opening of the THE HIGHER. new Bailey on Wednesday, CIVILIZATION. His Majesty the King made borne interesting and appro- priate remarks .relating to the more civilized methods in dealing with the criminal of to-day as compared with a century ago. "The barbarous penal code which was (homed ili-cessary one' hundred years ago;' his Majesty said, " has gradually been replaced in the progress towards a higher civilisation by laws breathing a more humane spirit and aiming at a nobler purpose. It is well that the criminal should be punished, but it is better that the criminal should be reformed." It seems to us that the case of Horace George Rayner—now so much in the public eye—presents a curious commentary on our " higher civilization." Here we have a young man who was seen to shoot the millionaire Mr. Whiteley and then turn the weapon upon himself with nearly fatal results. Had he succeeded in self-destruc- tion it would probably have been better. Instead, however, of being taken to the mortuary he was carried to the Infirmary and carefully nursed back to health. For what? To be hanged? If the jury finds him guilty of wilful murder he will presumably have to undergo capital punishment. Does it not seem a queer example of the" humane spirit" that we should drag a man back from the grave he sought in order to put him to the ordeal of trial for murder and then,possibly take away the life which we deliberately pre- served.? The fate of the murderer, in, the ordinary case, is just in the eyes of all but the disbelievers in capital punishment. The fate of Rayner we cannot presume, hut if he be hanged it must strengthen the arguments of those who confider that our higher civilization has not yet freed us from the methods of barbarism which we see fit to condemn in our ancestors.

Nothing has prepared the LAlch 'OD HOUSING. way for social reform more effectively than the Garden 9tY movement says the (" Daily New.") At the eighth annual meeting in London Sir Ralph Neville and Mr. Nettlefold dealt with two aspects of the ;I.uestion—land and housing. Sir Ralph urged the 'egielature to place our towns on the same footin.: as those of Germany in regard to the control of land around the cities. It cannot be too often stated that here is the secret of the extraordinary mini- eiPal Progress in Germany— a progress which astonishes the English visitor. While the Moder • atee in this country are attempting to strangle ~,ec'elmunal life and to deliver it over to th J.rusts, the German towns are advancing by leaps and bounds in the ownership of Public utilities, and, above all, in the ownership of land. Eleven hundred communities in Germany are exempt from rates because they own the land. Strasbourg owns 360 acres of land per head of its inhabitants, Berlin 82 acres, and so on. Not only so, but the German municipality can ear-mark, as it were, the surrounding land and take it out of the speculative market. What is the condition of things here? The towns are landless and helpless in the hands of the monopo- lists who lock up the land against the people until their needs compel them to acceptthe landlordi' terms. Then the town sprawls over into the country without any sort of central control, and new slums are generated on the outskirts to pro- vide new problems for the next generation. The housing question raised by Mr. Nettlefold is intimately associated with this subject of the land. Indeed, the further we go into the social problem, whether in town or country, the more certainly we find the land question at the root of all.

Captain Guest must be the Jonah of the Liberal party. The only two defeats sustained in the con- stituencies since the General Election have been at Cockermouth and Brigg, and in each case Captsin Guest was the Liberal standard-bearer. As he was also unsuccessful at the General Election when he stood for Kingswinford he has had the unique and unpleasant experience of being defeated three times in different constituencies in about twelve months. This should stiffice for the gallant captain and also for the Liberal Executive at headquarters. If he goes on fighting bye-elections at this or an increasing rate, he will seriously affect the balance of parties in the House.

* * We are pleased to note that the Street Improvement Committee has instructed the Borough Surveyor "to report upon all encroachments in streets caused by over-hanging signs and blinds, show cases, and goo Is exposed for sale. There are plenty of street obstruc- tions which tradesmen are responsible for. We could find any number, almost, of sun blinds and the like which are below the height required by the bye-laws. Show cases occasionally jog our elbows, but the worst encroachments are those which the butchers cause. Any pedestrian walking through our main streets on the footpath will find that he has every now and then to practically walk round the huge carcases of beasts which butchers hang over the pavement. They simply jostle the passer-by and, personally, we should prefer a lamp-post as being less greasy if harder. * * *

The gratification which the members of the War- rington Cycling Club naturally felt in reviewing a highly successful season on Thursday evening, was mingled with a sense of keen regret at the resignation after many years' untiring services of the Secretary-, Mr. E. Pickavance. No club could possibly have had a better secretary than the genial" Teddy,'-' who has thrown himself heart and soul into the many-sided work of the organization. His report on Thursday was, as usual, quite a model of its kind, the events of the season being vividly depicted with quite a wealth of poetic phraseology. The club has been happy in the choice of a successor, and we are confident that Mr. G. H. Smith will worthily uphold the high standard set by Mr. Pickavance. * * * Did anybody ever hear of the term "exaggerated ego " until he read it in the reports of the Thaw trial? A correspondent says : I used to hear it in a place where you may hear many unusual, obsolete and pedantic phrases—a certain tavern near the British Museum. If such a thing as Bohemianism ever existed in London it was as likely to be found there as any- where else. It was a place haunted by some of the regular readers of the Museum reading. room, and that is a queer society. But it was not from one of the customers at the tavern that I heard the phrase that the American alienist used. It was from a former landlady of the h ouse, the widow of a man who had been a famous writer on fishing. On a day after Dargai a bald- headed Scotsman took the floor to imitate bagpipes playing the "Cock o' the North," and an Irishman' also bald, was singing" The harp that once through Tara's halls." The landlady said, "You are both suffering from exaggerated ego, and I would knock your two heads together if I were not afraid of crack- ing them" which made me think that she believed the phrase to have something to do with eggs, and therefore with heads as bald as eggs.

_ Messrs. Gartons Limited, the well-known and enter- prising firm of seed specialists, have just issued an attractive publication giving particulars of their new and improved breeds of farm seeds for 1907. Agricul- turalists would do well to consult the list which affords a mass of valuable information presented in a con- venient form. The firm, which was established in 1880, has long devoted special attention to the raising and perfecting of agricultural seeds, and it is worthy of note that during the past year many first prizes and other awards have been gained with the produce of Messrs. Garton's new and. improved breeds of seeds, both at home and abroad. All seeds quoted in the new catalogue are offered under a guarantee of purity and germination, and subject to the analysis of the botanist of the Royal Agricultural Society of Etkland, the Highland and Agricultural Society of Scotland, and the Department of Technical Instruction for Ireland or any other botanists or experts of recognised authority. To meet the increasing demand for their seeds, Messrs Gartoas have during the past year been obliged to erect still more extensive seed-cleaning machinery which has been constructed upon the latest principles. The book contains numerous illustrations which include samples of produce raised from the sends and views of the firm's experimental grounds at Acton Grange. There is also an article giving a botanical analysis of the seeds of a clover plant and the firm are to be complimented upon issuing such an excellent and nforming book.

Shorthand Writers' Association. The usual weekly meeting of this Association was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Wednesday last, a fair number of members attending. Speed practice was conducted in two sections—high and low rates after which Mr. Craddock read a federation lecture entitled "Poetry in relation to Life." The writer was of opinion that, even in this rampant age of com- mercialism poetry might be found a periennial source of pleasure. Most of us, however, were so much imbued with the spirit of twentieth-centuriam, that really thoughtful people were comparatively few, and in this stirging age of competitive trust and combine, it was, perhaps, palpably unjust to expect other- wise. He considered, however, that the more thoughtful we became, the more we found our selves drawn towards the shaping and refining influence of poetry, which was a subject intensely calculated to assume an influence in our thoughts and actions. He could recommend nothing finer than a thoughtful perusal of his best poetry in our noble literature. Often in the midst of monotonous routine we very naturally sought a refuge in anything that offered entertainment and diversion to our jaded mind and fagged, brain, and he thought poetry was a very goad tonic towards driving away such jadedness and brain fag; and that it had intrinsic powers of influence to act either as a soothing palliative or a stimulant. From a photographer's point of view, the lecturer con- sidered that although as a rule shorthand writers were very practical people,no one could pose as an educated all-round shorthand writer unless he had a fair acuuaintance with poetry. The interesting paper was much enjoyed, and a discussion ensued, the meeting terminating with the customary foto of thanks.

Mr. J. A. Yong', the eldest son of the Rev. John Yonge, is to be congratulated ott. the results of the Cambridge University Local Examinations, held in December, which show that thirty•five pupils of Silooates School, Saltburn (of which he is Master) were successful, and that of these fifteen were placed in the Honours Divisions. The Last Chance. The last two performances of, " Aladdin " at the Liverpool Royal Court Theatre take place to-day. There will be a a matinee at 2 o'clock and the final performance at 7 o'clock. This pantomime is undoubtedly one of the best seen in Lancashire for many years and has been wonderfully sudeessful. Harry Tate, Malcolm Scott, Hetty King and Fanny Fields are as amusing as ever, and those who have not seen them should take this final opportunity if they can make it at all possible.

Chicago Scandals.—Grossly Exaggerated. The series of lectures arranged by the Warrington Co-operative Society was continued on Thursday, when a lecture was given by Mr. A. W. Lobb, Manchester, in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. Mr. Rushton presided over a crowded attendance. Mr. Lobb dealt principally with the salmon and fruit canning industries of British Columbia and California, illustrating by means of a splendid series of lantern slides the various processes. Incidentally he referred to the recent scandals in .regard to the Chicago packing trade. Twelve months ago, he said, the people of England were startled by a nutnber of newspaper articles descriding the dreadful things which were happening in Chicago in regard to the tinned meat trade. Those articles were grossly exaggerated if not absolutely untrue. He did not say that the tinned goods were absolutely above suspicion but they were not nearly so bad as they were painted,and,in fact, the contents of many of the tins which were con- demned in England, were returned to America, and then eaten without bad results. But whatever might be said about Chicago goods, nothing could be said about the tinned salmon and fruit. A brief cinemato- graph display concluded the proceedings.
