Warrington Builder's Bankruptcy.

At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yesterday, before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, Walter Leigh, builder, Orford-lane, and carr:ying on business in Dutton-st., appeared for his public examination. The dehtnrs's statement showed liabilities 'amounting to £593 14s. 3d and the assets were estimated to produce £212 18s., leaving a deficiency of £3BO 16s. 3d. He attributed his failure to loss on contracts, lack of capital, sickness of children, and bad weather. On the 18th October, last he entered into a contract with Mr. J. T. Barton to erect twenty-five houses, and one house and shop for £2,821. Eight houses had been partially erected, five were stated to be nearly completed, and the bankrupt had received £370 on account of work, done. Bankrupt stated that the value of work done on the houses in question was £620, £250 more than he received.—Bankrupt was subjected to a long examination by Mr. Grant Gibson Official receiver) and stated that he had no capital when 4re started business and never hiatny capital during his trading. As far as he knew helwas solvent when he entered up on the large contract referred to. The price of 108 Ios. per house was not a fair one, considering the cost of materials. This unfortu- nate job was practically the sole cause of his failure. He admitted that on the day before he filed his petition Messrs. Clare and Ridgway carted away a considerable quantity of materials which they had supplied on the job, but said it was because he failed to let them have a cheque which he had 'mortised them. It was not at that time known that he (debtor) had given instruc- tions for the filing of his petition and he had not informed Messrs. Clare and Ridgway to that effect.—The examination was closed.

Science Students' Association A meeting of the above association was held on Saturday evening, Mr. Robson 'in the chair. Mr. J. Allan, F.C.S., of Messrs. Crosgeld .and Sons, Ltd., read a paper, "0n the Liquefaction of Gases: with a Demonstration of the Properties of Liquid Air." The lecturer gave a short history of the attempts made since the commencement of the 19th century to liquefy gases,reviewing the work of Faraday, Black, Thilorier, and the theoretical considerations with regard to critical temperature and pressure estab- lished by Andrews in 1869 which led to the success in 1877 of Cailettet and Pictet in the Liquefaction of Oxygen. At this point sealed tubes containing liquid carbon dioxide at its critical point were shown. Ooming to later times he dealt with the researches of Wroblewski, Olszewski and Prof. Dewar which resulted in the manufacture of liquefied gases in quantity, and described the machines constructed for that purpose la) Linde and Ilampson. Then followed a demonstration of the remarkable properties of liquid air. The profound changes brought about by immers- ing vegetable, metallic or gaseous substances in liquid air were here shown by a series of very interesting experiments, and the recent application of liquid air for the production of high vacuo was also demon- strated. rhe experiments performed to illustrate its properties were very numerous, the general result being to freeze the substance placed in it. A daffodil placed in it crumbled to pieces on being afterwards hit against a board. Coal-gas, ether, carbon, dioxide, ST., were frozen into a white solid mass. A lead bell, after being subjected to the action of liquid air rang with quite a silvery ring, while quicksilver was frozen into solid and could afterwards be hammered out. India-rubber placed in it for a minute became as brittle as glass, while cotton wool became more explosive than gun-cotton. The interest aroused by the lecture was shown by the numerous questions asked at its conclusion. A vote of thanks, moved by Mr. Fisher, and seconded by Mr. Newton was carried with acclamation.

The Late Councillor Brough.

The will of the late Councillor Brough, Fearnbead has been proved at £5,245.

A Village Centenary. With the above title Mr. Arthur Mounfield has just issued a nicely-printed and illustrated booklet relating the history of the Independent Methodist Church, Stockton Heath from 1500 to 1906. The booklet once again tells the tale of the formation of the first total abstinence society in England at Stockton Heath, and incontesttbly proves the local claim to priority in the cause of total abstinence. The sale price of the booklet, which has only a limited issue, is 6d. and copies may be obtained from Mr. Afounfield or from the Independent Methodist Book Room, Wigan.

Local Eisteddfod Successes. At Widnes Eisteddfod on Thursday two local successes were achieved. The Apollo Male Voice quartette (Messrs. Cook, Critchley, Gittens and Wood- cock) obtained the prize of Id in the male quartette competition, the test piece being " Robin Adair" (Cantor). The adjudicator said it was a long time since he heard such a good quartette, the tone being beautiful. Stockton Heath Wesleyan Choir are to be congratulated on obtaining the first prize of six guineas and a guinea for the conductor in the mixed choral competition. The test selection was " How amiable are Thy tabernacles," and the choir sang it very admirably under the conductorship of Mr. F. E. Penketh.

WOOD CEIIMNEY PIECES, Fire Grates and Tiles

unrivalled seletion, inspection invitel.—J. S. GARLAND, Builders' Ironmonger, &c., 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. 2nd South Lancashire Hand at the Parr Hall.

The band of the 2nd South Lancashirs Regiment brought their visit to Warrington to a close on Sun- day afternoon with a sacred concert in the Parr Hall. As the proceeds were in aid of the War Memorial Fund, it was more than gratifying to see the hall crowded with an appreciative audience. The pro- gramme was one of unusual interest and the per- formers undoubtedly proved themselves to be a baud of the first rank. The chief item was Tschaikowsky's Grande Overture Solennelle " 1812" which is descrip- tive of the invasion of Russia in 1812 by the French under Napoleon I. and their final defeat. It is a brilliant and most intricate composition, but the band rose to the occasioa4and their interpretation delighted the audience. The close of the piece was particularly effective, the jubilation of the victotious Russians finding vent in the firing of guns and ringing of church bells. Another deliOtful feature of the concert was Winterbottom s arrangement of " Reminiscences of Mendelssohn," which introduced a number of the great composer's best-known works, and svas given with great taste and expression. The stirring pro- cessional march "Silver Trumpets" (Viviani) was played with fine spirit, and a pleasing diversion was the intermezzo Cathedral Chimes " (Lindahl), introducing a peal of tubular bells. Mention must also be made of a thoroughly adequate interpretation of the Austrian hymn with variations, and of the concluding selection "I' Pagliacci " (Leoncavalli) which was quite a triumph of execution. A cornet solo "The Children's Home" (Cowen). by Band Sergeant W. Boots, was exceedingly well played, and called forth warm applause. Colour-Sergeant Broad- hurst, a Warringtonian, scored a particularly striking success with the song " Star of Bethlehem," which he randered with much feeling, and he responded to an encore with "The Holy City." Before the close of the performance the band played The Last Post" in memory of departed comrades. Mr. Andrew Harris, L.R.A.M., conducted, and he is to be specially complimented upon the excellent manner in which the band acquitted itself.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DEATH OF MR. H. CORRIN.

We regret te record the death, which took plice after a painfully short illness on Friday evening of last week of Mr. Henry Corrin, of Rutland-street, Runcorn. He was 58 years of age. So recently as Tuesday he was at work. Congestion of the lungs followed a chill, and the skilled attendion of Dr. Robinson proved of no avail The late Mr. Corrin came to Runcorn from Grenaby, Isle of Man, over 30 years ago, and during the whole of that period lived in the Dukes Fields district. He was in the employ as blacksmith for many years of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. He was well-known as a clever worker in steel, brass and copper, and developed his penchant for the hobby in his leisure time. Personally he was au unobtrusive and kindly dispositioned man, enjoying much popularity amoag those who knew him, an his untimely death is greatly deplored. The funeral took place at Runcorn Cemetery on Tuesday.

WIDNES GAS CHALLENGED. The town of Widnes has taken up very seriously the suggestion that Sheffield supplies gas more cheaply, and Mr. Isaac Carr, the manager of the Corporation's gas works, says that though the excellent administration of the Sheffield Gas Company has for years past com- manded the admiration of all interested in the gas industry, it is not yet that Widnes has to be relegated to the second place. Warrington Society The annual meeting of the Warrington Society was held at the Old Academy on Wednesday evening, Mr. L. Greening presiding over a good attendance. Alder- man Bennett in his annual report stated that the membership stood at 124, as compared with 133 a year ago, and the financial position was steadily improving. Referring to the proposed purchase by the' Corporation of St. Elphin's Park he said the committee felt that it would be a public calamity to allow that ancient, picturesque and healthy spat to be covered with houses. Mr. William Long was re-elected president and Alderman Bennett and Mr. E. J. Hall (hon. secretaries), and Mr. Fairhurst (hon. treasurer)were re-appointed to their respective offices. The executive committee was re-appointed with the exception that Mr. Charles Madeley and Mr. P. C. Garnett replaced Mr. J.E. Birtles and Mr. J. Langland, the two gentlemen to retire in rotation. Death of Mrs. J. R. Buckton. We regret to record the death of Mrs. J. 11. Buckton (widow of the late Mr. James Rhoades Buckton, solicitor, Warrington), which took place at her resi- dence at Whitehurch on Friday of last week. The deceased lady, who was 77 years of age, formerly resided at Warrington, and took an active interest in church work in connection with the Parish, church and missions and also in St. Peter's parish, and did good service by her connection with the Ladies' Clxarity, the Clergy Daughters' School, and the Ladies' School, now Smith-street School. After her husband's death she took up her residence at Whitchurch, where she also engaged in church work as long as her health permitted, and where, as at Warrington, she was deeply respected. She leaves two sons (of whom Colonel Buckton is one) and two daughters to mourn her loss. The remains of the deceased lady were interred in the Cemetery at Warrington on Monday afternoon, amid many manifestations of respect and esteem.

Wesleyan Congregational Gathering.

The annual congregational gathering in connection with Bewserroad Wesleyan Church was held on Thursday evening and was a pronounced success. The proceedings opened with a tea to which about 180 sat clown, their wants being attended to by lady members of the congregation. Subsequently an enjoyable concert was held in the church, Mr. .T. W. Hayward presiding over a large attendance. Songs were ably rendered by the Misses Lester and Fletcher and Mr. Cede; a duet by Messrs. Tynan and Pearce, and part songs by the choir. Mr., Pearce presided at the organ and contributed several excellent voluntaries. During the evening Mrs. Pitt distributed some 80 handsome book presents to Sunday sehool scholars for regular attendance. In his annual report of the work in the Sun- day school, Mr. Maddock highly praised the efforts of the teachers. They had on the books 478 scholars with an average attendance, in the morning of 156 and in the afternoon of 287. In 1905 the number of children on the registers was 438. Financially the year 1906 concluded with a balance in hand of £2 Bs. 4d. Handball.

Under the auspices of the Liverpool and District Handball League, Port Sunlight visited the Borough Gymnasium,,Palmyra Square on Thursday night, and engaged in an exciting game with the Warrington team. The game opened fast and furious the tackling on both sides being very lFeen,with the result that several fouls were committed one against the soap team within the penalty area, but Warrington failed to score. Immediately after, some good passing amongst the home team forwards resulted in Topping scoring. The game continued at a quick rate, and later Woods registered the 2nd goal for the home team. The 2nd half saw the game resumed as vigorous by as before,and one of the Port Sunlight men being tackled in a rough manner rather dazed him for a few minutes. However the soap team were not long after before they regis- tered a goal, and had very hard lines later with one or two good throws. From some further very quick movements, Woods secured the 3rd goal for the home team. The Warrington backs were always safe, and the forwards did some good passing, but should have secured one or two more goals from the number of throws they had. Final result: —Warrington 3 goals, Port Sunlight 1 goal- Warrington journey to Egremont next Wednesday night, where they are engaged in a league match with Egremont St. John's:

WARRINGTON LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the General Council of the Warrington Liberal Association was held at the Liberal Club on Wednesday evening, Mr. C. E. Parker presiding over an exceptionally large attendance. The following officers were elected : —President, Mr. C. E. Parker; vice-presidents, Messrs. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., Frank A. Roberts, Ziba Armitage, Edwin J, Guest, Alderman Pierpoint, Henry Roberts, Alderman Tinnion, and William Madden ; treasurer, Mr. William Furness; secretary, Mr. J. T. Locker. The annual meeting of the Executive Committee was afterwards held, Councillor James Evans pre- siding. The Committee re-elected Councillor Evans as chairman, and Councillor Wilkinson as deputy- chairman. The forthcoming Board of Guardians elections were discussed, and it was decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Ward Committees.

ST. HELENS STREET FATALITY.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquiry at St. Helens, on Tuesday into the death of James Bicker- staffe, aged eleven, of 11, Crab-street, who died at the Providence Hospital on Friday from injuries to the I head. It appears that on Tuesday afternoon last week Bickerstaffe and another boy raced away along Forndryl street, and when they got to the corner of St. Mary's- street they were met by a butcher's cart, which ran over the boy.—The boy's father said he saw the lad in hospital before he became unconscious. He told him he was rrlning and slipped off his feet and fell under the horse, and the wheel went over his forehead.— Richard Roberts, butcher, of 82, Higher Parr-street,rlid he was pulling up to go round the corner of St. Mary's- street into Foundry-street when the lad ran across in front. He tried to pull up, but in doing so slipped on his back r Ider the horse. %Vitra( -a picked the lad up. and took him to the doctor. The lad at the time told hits he was not to blame.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned and the driver was exonerated from blame.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe Congregational Church.

A large congregation assembled at the above church on Sunday morning,when an interesting and instructive address was delivered by the Pastor, the Rev. John Yunge, on "The quest for God—its inspiration and encouragement." In the evening the preacher was the Rev. Hugh A. Ellis, of Manchester' secretary of the Religious Tract Society. Mr. Ellis during his address entered into detail in regard to the work of this excel- lent society,interesting and convincing statistics being adduced. The usual offertory was taken at each service, and a retiring collection on behalf of the Religious Tract 'Society was made and realized a good amount.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A.

There was a good attendance at the lastjmeeting for the financial year of the Wycliffe Society. The Hon. Secretary presided. Mr. E. Boreland, of Liverpool, agent of the Alliance, gave an address upon "This or that : a small penny loaf or a glass of whisky," showing the great advantage with its comparative cheapness of bread, and the injurious effect of intoxicating drinks both financially and physically. Mr. Boreland, who is an expert on the drink question and local veto made his address very plain and forcible. Miss A. Bridge sang in a very able manner "0 Love Divine" and "Harbour Lights." Mr. A. Doses and Mr. D. Owen also took part in the meeting.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Rev Paul Bull on Gambling.

,In the course of his address on "The life of man "at the Warrington Parish Church, on Friday evening the Rev. Rev. Paul Bull, contended that gambling was a more serious vice that lust, and more destructive than drink. The GIPS° of gambling, the effect, and the cure were dealt with. Gambling was not wrong in itself,but because of what it led to. There was, the beginning of sin. Men gambled for the love of money, and the excitement,and because of the drudgery and hard work of life, but he was in hopes that by a special reconstruc- tion of the hours of toil they would win for every man in Eggland a fairer and more human life, when labour for him, as it was for the clergy, would be a pure joy of the highest nature. He included the Stock Exchange in his rernarks,as well as a small bet in a "pub," because there was in each case the same desire for money and excitement, and the desire to get money without work- ing for it, and, therefore,it was dishonest, led to prisor, , destroyed the foundations of character, the spirit of industry, and faith in God and man. There was only one cure, and that was religion, which brought the will of God to bear on the will of man. It was by prayer, diligent and earnest prayer, that the cure could be effected.

Before concluding his sermon, the preacher paid a graceful tribute to General Buller, who, he said, had visited Warrington on the previous day, and no doubt had had a good reception. The General was very dear to him, because he (the speaker) had been his chaplain. He had met him since the war, and knew something about him. In suite of the misrapresentations, mis- fortunes, and misunderstandings which had gathered round his name, General Buller was still dear to him, as be was the hero of all who had served under his com- mand. He wished the congregation to mark why it was that all who had served under him loved him with such loyol devotion: it was forth° reason that he had always been ready to risk losing his life to save the life of a comrade.

WARRINGTON MALE VOICE CHOIR.

Concert in Parr* Hall.

The sixth annual concert given by the Warrington Male Voice Choir on Saturday evening fully sustained the good reputation the choir has won in recent years. Its own work, in the rendering of a number of com- positions which formed the "test pieces "at com- petitions within the last three months at Preston and Warrington, was of so finished a character that the choir's ultimate attainment of first place amongst its rivals would seem to be a matter of patient and con- tinued application only. Whether under Mr. Nesbitt's or Mr. Hassall's baton the choir proved equally amen- able to control and exhibited a most praiseworthy concentration and reliance upon the conductor's guid- ance. Amongst the items ,which I,requent repetition has not made too familiar " The Hungarian March " (Dc Rille) and the part-songs " What care 1 " (Blumenthal) and "Breathe soft ye winds" (Calkin) may be mentioned as the choicest specimens. The taking up of the disjointed parts in the first mentioned and the taste and precision shewn in the two others were alike admirable and musicianly, all these pieces evidencing a degree of intelligent insight into their meaning which is as gratifying as it is seldom met with. Of Madame Jessie Hulme's two songs nothing which is not commendatory need be said. Her vocal- isation of "The Jewel Song " especially, was both ST. FATALITY AT VIADUCT WORKS:

An inquest was held on Monday afternoon by the Deputy Coroner, Mr. Jones, on the body of Morris Davies, blacksmith, of Rathbone-street, who met with an accident whilst following his employment in the Viaduct Works. Arthur Grissold, a fellow workman, proved that the injury had been caused by a piece of iron falling on his foot. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

"NAPOLEON THE GREAT."

The fourth Gilchrist Lecture was given on Monday evening, the lecturer being Mr. .1. Holland Rose, Litt. D. (Camb.), Mr. W. Collingwood presiding. The subject was "Napoleon the Great.' The lecturer referred to the rise and fall of Napoleon, his ancestry and character, and showed how he was influenced by the ! customs of Corsica. His early belief in the doctrines iof the French Revolution and how he scattered the i malcontents of Paris.in 1795 were touched upon. The first effort of Napoleoa to overthrow the British Empire in the East was in 1798 when Nelson was in command, and the Battle of the Nile was fought, and Napoleon's fleet was annihilated, hut he escaped to France and was , made first Consul. His next great victory was at the ?Battle of Austerlitz against the Russians and Austrians in 1805. And in the same year he suffered another great defeat at the Battle of TrafalFar. Mr. Rose gave a Nil description of Napoleon's device to exclude Great Britainl from trading with continental Europe. IRON AND STEEL BUILDERS' CONCERT.

The members of the Boiler Makers and Irom and . Steel Ship Builders' (Newton-le-Willows Branch) ' I Association, held a smoking concert on Saturday, at their headquarters the Sunbeam Inn, for the purpose of presenting three emblems, the gifts of the Executive Council to Bros. H. S. Turner, E. Jolley and A. Jolley. I Bro. W. Davies, jun., presided, and was supported by Bro. J. Lovell (district auditor), and Bro. J. Cordingley, • and branch offices. Bros. J. The manager of a manufacturing firm was watching a carman tugging at a heavy case one day. The man's face was red, and the muscles of his neck were standing out. The manager thought it was the right moment to offer practi- cal assistance. " Wait a minute, there," he said. "Let me show you how easy things be- come when you use a little brain with your muscle." He took up a hook, stuck it into a case, gave a pull, and went sprawling into the gutter. He got up, looked at the hook, and said: Con- found it The handle has come off." "Yes. sir;" said the carman.- " My brain told me that, and I didn't use it."

Two Norwegian brothers were employed in Canada in floating logs over a waterfall. It was dangerous work, but they took it calmly and patiently, until one day the elder walked into the company's office. " I dink," he said " you better send anooder man to help me on dat va'rfall." "Veil, my bruder he fall in de vater dis mornin' an' go over de va'rfall, an' I haf not seen him since, an' I dink he be goin' to qvit." Maving thus done his whole duty to his employers, the man returned to his work.

At the beginning of the recent Russo-Japa- nese war a schoolmaster told a class of boys the cause of the fighting, and then asked all who favoured the war to hold up their hands. Up went every hand but one. "Well, Jack, why are you opposed to the war?" "Cause. sir, war makes history, an' there's more now'n I can ever learn," was the totally unexpected answer of the youngster.

"Did you ever drive a donkey, Fitz.?" "Yes, sir." "What did you say when you wished him to stop?" " Whoa !" The sergeant turned away, and immediately put his squad in motion. After they had advanced a dozen yards or so he bawled out at the top of his lungs: " Squad halt! Whoa, Fitzgerald ! "

The Examiner, Saturday, March 2, 1907.

who were able and willing to work without the means of earning the wherewithal to secure the necessaries of life. They had

EVERY RIGHT TO BE DISSATISFIED with these products of their industrial system. Was it any wonder that men were willing to embrace anything which promised relief ? They had Socialism with its large promises, its proposals to sweep away individual possession, individual capital, and to establish a system whereby everyone was to get what, he needed, whatever be his contribution. Socialism in its constructive form (in so far us it had been defined) was unworkable, and conflicted with permanent characteristics of the human species. But he recognized that it was quite futile to prove by elaborate logical argument that Socialism in its constructive form was no care for present industrial sores. The demand for a way out or existing wrongs which oppressed the poor and needy, arose in a great murmur of a jastified dis- content an became articulate in the demand, and the question," If you consider Socialism would involve the people in conditions worse than they escape from, what methods do you propose as an escape from conditions which on your own acknowledgement are producing results which are a disgrace to any system ?" Escape could he found, and found alone, in the abolishing of individual ownership of land. If there could be a con- centration of all progressive forces, call them by what name they liked, upon the land question, it would do more than any other reform to do away with the glaring inequalities in the distribution of wealth,and the misery and destitution at one end of the social scal..s. Tt was a reform which appealed to reason, and was based on economic lam and social well-being. It would not regenerate the world or bring happiness to everyone, but it would go very far to establish equality of oppor- tunity to all, a thing every well-wisher of his country should desire to bring about. (Applause.)

LAND, LABOUR & CAPITAL.

MR. C E. PARKER ADDRESSES THE PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE.

Land Nationalization Advocated.

Mr. C. E. Parker delivered an interesting address 011 "Land, labour and capital" at ihe meeting of the Pro- gressive Labour League held at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening. Mr. Thomas Barnes presided over a fair attendance.

Mr. PARKER said it would be readily acknowledged by everyone that the establishment of the right rela- tions between the ideas conveyed by these three terms was'of the utmost importance, and that they might be able to think clearly, it was essential that they should clearly understand what they meant when they used them. in economics the term" land" was applied to all natural elements. It included land with its soil, its minerals, the air, light, water, in a word every material thing or force that God had made. Labour was simply work. It included both the work of the head and of the hand. Capital was stored up labour. It was labour impressed on matter so that matter •would minister to the gratifi- cation of human desire. To borrow a definition : land, labour, and capital were the factors of production. The term land included all natural opportunities or forces; , the term labour, all human exertion; and the term ' capital, all wealth used to produce more wealth. In returns to these three factors was the whole prodnce distributed. That part which went to landowners as payment for the use of natural opportunities was called rent; and that part which constituted the reward of human exertion was called wages ; and that part which constituted the return for the use of capital was called interest keeping these definitions clearly in mind it would at once occur to them that they were very loosely used in their every-day affairs. The injustice of private ownership in land had been clearly admitted in connec- t , to.:in with the land values surrounding growing towns. I The fortunate owners of such land were enabled to

===== 0002601_19070302_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
SIT TIGHT AND DO NOTHING

simply to wait until the growth of the town compelled the inhabitants to pay their price. As a remedy for that it was proposed by some, who thought themselves advanced, to tax what were called "site values." The owner of a piece of land let it for, say agricultural purposes, and it was worth for that purpose £lOO an acre and bore a rent of £3, but the owner would not sell it under £5OO for building purposes. It was sug- gested that that land should be taxed on its selling value. To his mind that injustice had been too much treated as if it were an isolated accidental injustice springing out of the gathering together of the popula- tion in cities. The Liberal Party were loudly crying out for more legislation for that particular injustice. To his mind it would be far wiser to recognize that as evidence not of an isolated wrong, but a wrong which was possible whenever there was private ownership of natural elements, and out of the recognition of that fact to direct the attention of the people to the broad principle which taught that private ownership of natural products involved always and everywhere an injustice. An appreciation of that fact would result in an universal demand to have all natural elements handed over to the control of the State to be held in trust for the good of all. To his mind that was the outcome of the recogni- tion of the principle that a man was only entitled to what he himself produced, and the private ownership of what he had never produced

===== 0002601_19070302_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
INVOLVED AN INJUSTICE

to his fellows. If that unearned increment belonged to those who produced it, it ought to be appropriated for their use and not merely taxed. If they had to begin de novo all would agree that in civilization sufficiently strong to be just, State control over natural elements should be assumed. There was no doubt that present conditions bad made the problem very complicated by the fact that genuine capital which had been honestly made by labour had been in many cases exchanged for the right to exercise control over land, and that made the righting of the wrong very difficult. They were very familiar with the phrase "Ile tyranny of the capitalist system." He suggested that at least nine- tenths of that tyranny was not the tyranny of capital at all but arose from the land monopoly, or, that capital was able to appear in the role of a tyrant because it could entrench itself behind the land monopoly. They often beard the expression that capital should be the servant of labour, and not labour the servant of capital. He quite agreed, and that which made capital in many cases" boss" was because it had its foothold in the ownership of land. The struggle between capital and labour was like a tug-of-war in which the capitalist side had their end of the rotoe, unseen by their oppon- ents, fastened to the ground. (Laughter.) Capital derived a tremendous power when it could root itself in land monopoly. Now suppose

TIM LAND WAS TO RN HRLD IN TRUST

for all the workers,instead of kicking their heels on the pavement and whistling to keep their courage up, they could go to the Government and treat with them for access to mother earth. They all knew what great quantities of land there were in this country which only required labour being expended on thein to bring them in a condition in which cultivation would become remunerative. There were vast tracts at land that would grow timber; there were flats at present practically unproductive which by draining could be made to yield their increase. He was not advocating the employment by Government of those on strike specially becanse they were on strike, but that labour apart altogether from the reasons which had caused the need should always have the opportunity of turning to the mother ot them all, the earth, which was ever ready to yield her sustenance in exchange for that labour. But the workers would have a further alternative instead of having to stand idly byand watch with anxious hearts their accumulated savings, sometirns slowly, but always surely, dwindle *may in providing for food and lodgings for their families. Instead of that system of slow starvation, there would be the opportunity for their workers com- bining their saving and associating their effort in com- mencing a business of their own. Under present condi- tions it was very difficult for associated workmen to commence an industrial undertaking,and for that reason amongst others,in consequence of individual ownership, all the best claims had been staked out and appropriated. He said claims had been staked out, but that did not in any way imply, that these claims were being worked. Many of these were sppropriated not with the inten- tion of working them, but solely with the idea of preventing other people working them. That Illustrated what he had already put forward, that capital was enabled to play the part of the tyrant when it could get the help of the land monopoly. Mr. Parker gave an illustration to show theta capitalist company, being allowed to monopolise natural products, could exclude all except itself and work that monopoly for its own benefit. Capital itself would be practically harmless, but when it was able to fasten is root in the land monopoly then it could exercise its power tyrani- cally. They were accustomed to hear of the

MONOPOLY OF CAPITAL, but capital was only stored-up labour. There was no monopoly in labour because everyone could give it forth ; the only way in which capital could become a monopoly was through the material things in which it was stored being monopolised. Labour must be impressed upon matter before it could assume the permanent form of capital. If they adopted the principle that everyone should have equal access to mother earth then there could be no monopoly in the materials in which labour was stored. Equal access to mother earth did not by any means imply an equal division of the earth's so rface.Such a plan would not only be impossible but also unfair, inasmuch as some parts of the earth were much more valuable than others. They did not claim equal portions of land, but rather that the State should aot as trustee of the land for its people, and that it should equally distribute the income arising from the rents for the natural products for which there had been established an equality of opportunity for all. That being established it then remained foreach one to make the best of his individual opportunity. They would, he thought, recogaize clearly the difference between those proposals and those which were generally taught by Socialists. He repeated what he bad already stated in dealing with these ideas elsewhere, what he contended should be

THE AIM OF ALL SOCIAL LAW " thata man should get his produce, his whole produce and nothing but his produce." He advocated the nationalization of natural products because he recog- nized that individual ownership of them created con- ditions which conflicted with that elementary right. Headvocated the nationalization of land, or what might possibly answer the purpose and appeal more readily to on: English genius of social advancement, the passing of such measures as would enable the people to derive all the benefits arising out of the possession and use of natural elements at present going to individual owners. Dealing with the question of interest, Mr. Parker said the earnings of capital could best be expressed in money, and might be put down at the present time at from three to four per cent, per annum. All over and above this, apart from the labour element, arose from two things, flrst from the necessity of providing for the possible loss of the capital and was like an insurance fund ; the second was the result of monopoly. They. might take it that the average earnings of capital In industrial affairs was 6 per cent. But that took no account of the losses of the capital itself ; the 2 per cent. which was over and above the normal 4 formed a kind of insurance fund against the risk of capital loss. It was quite true that some business concerns were doing much better that' It might arise either from the undertakings Possessing a monopoly in some form; or from the labour and skill of the workers from the managing bead downwatds being insufficiently remunerated. It must be freely acknowledged that the present industrial system while as a whole it enabled those engaged in it to live in comfort and to 110111 c extent luxury, there was a considerable number at the base of the industrial ladder, to whom the getting of their daily bread was one long weary struggle, the end of which was often the poorhouse and the pauper's grave. That did not arise except in a limited number of cases from unwillingness to work, and no one could rest content with a system which, while it on the one hand pro- duced viealth in great abundance, left many of those

===== 0002601_19070302_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DISCUSSION.

Mr. J. MORTON said the mere fact of the community owning the land values would not ensure that every individual would receive an equivalent share of them. The proceeds might go into the coffers of the State and still the same conditions as to poverty, &c. might remain. He did not see how under any system of dealing with the land alone they were going to alter the fundamental conditions of society. Mr. OCKEL strongly advocated the single tax system and pointed out that if it were adopted, land which at present was kept back by speculation or used for unpro- ductive purposes would be opened up to the people. Captain CROSFIELD said he was not quite clear as to how far the people who advocated the taxation of land values wished to go. Would the system apply to all land—agricultural as well as suburban? Many people said they quite agreed to preventing speculation in land in the neighbourhood of towns, which raised the rents and caused congestion, but they did not believe in the splitting up of the parks in the country districts. Personally be was not sure whether these large landed estates were not in a great many instances of great value to the country. Mr. C. Las said Mr. Parker had suggested the unity of all progressive forces to bring about land nationaliza- tion. Was the scheme likely to be acceptable to the Liberal party, if it was officered and led by capitalists and landowners ?

Mr. G. OSBORN/I thought that if a man could afford to keep a park he should pay for the luxury. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Parker, on the proposition of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. SCRAGG.

Mr. PARKER said he had been asked if be expected the Liberal party to take up this reform. He was not very particular whom`he expected. If it, was a right thing, people should go for it whether they were Tories, Socialists, Liberals, or anything else. A party name was a means to an end, and they must be careful that they did not make it an end in itself. He believed the rise in land values was one reason shy they had cycles in trade.

MESSRS. RICHMOND'S EXTENSION.

HANDSOME NEW PREMISES AT GRAPPENHALL

During the past week the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., have commenced manufacturing at their new Foundry at Grappenhall and marked another epoch in the career of the Company. For some years past the works at Academy-street have been incapable of adequately dealing alone with the requirements of the growing business and the works at Stratford (London) have also been taxed to their utmost capacity. Hemmed in on all sides, it was impossible to carry out any further extensions at Academy-street and the site at Grappenhall was secured with the view of concentrating under one roof the whole of the manufacturing departments of the business. The site has been well chosen, on the Warrington side of the Ship Canal (near Latchford Locks) and is conveniently situated for the speedy and economical handling of a large traffic—both inwards and outwards. Covering 91 acres, it allows ample space for future extensions. Building operations were only commenced eight months ago and in that short space of time the foundry has ben erected and completely equipped with the most up-to- date plant machinery and appliances. A large portion of the machinery has been made at the Academy-street Works and the whole of it has been designed by and erected under the supervision of Mesers.R ichmond's own engineeringstaff. Every convenience has been provided on an elaborate scale with the view of increasing the production of the foundries. The core ovens are of an original design which the company has introduced for the first time with the intention of quickly and thoroughly drying the cores. A novel feature is the I extra accommodation for the storage of cores not !required for immediate use. In this department a large 1 portion of the employees are females and their comfort ' and convenience have been carefully studied both as 1 regards working conditions and the sanitary arrange- ments. Power is derived from a double plant of large gas engines, which can be worked together or separately, without stoppage. The buildings are 1 lofty, well ventilated and well-ligt.ted, and are fitted throughout with the latest high pressure incandescent gas lamps. A pntiful supply of water is obtained from a well,sunk in convenient position in the Foundry and the large area of roofing has been utilised for supple- menting the supply, whenever it may be necessary, by I means of tank storage. _ _

The formal opening of the foundry took place on Thursday of last week when the works were visited by the Directors of he Company and a trial cast made in their presence. On Friday the whole of the representu- tives of the Company were assembled in Warrington for the bi-annual Conference and advantage was taken of the occasion for the inspection of the new premises. The party was conducted by Mr. John A. Ransome, the Managing Director in charge of the works, and was accompanied by Mr. H. M. Thornton, vice-chairman and managing director, Mr. J. W. Glover, director, Mr. James W. Slack, secretary, and a large party of influen- tial friends, as well as the representatives, their assist- ants ad the heads of departments. On arrival at the works. Mr. Ransome briefly explained the circumstances which had led to the new departure—the limitations of their existing facilities and the urgent necessity for in- creased and more economical production. The present foundry space, he said, covered an area of nearly 10,000 square yards, with accomodation for several hundred men. The buildings bud been erected with the view of being easily extended as occasion required without interfering in any way with the work of production. Promptitude in despatch of orders had always been one of their chief aims and notwithstanding the great increase in the volume of business they would now be able to fully maintain the reputation they had gained for quick delivery. Many things which were done by hand in the old foundry would now be done by machinery and well-known as they always had been for the soundness and fine finish of their castings, with the additional space and extra conveniences available they wonld now be able to make still further improvements in this respect. The men from the old foundry were being moved to the new in small parties and the change was thus being made without any cessation in the output. After a tour of inspection had been completed, each member of the party made a trial cast. These will be finished in the Company's patent "Porcellanite "enamel and are intended to be treasured as souvenirs.

The opening of these new works cannot fail to be of benefit to the town of Warrington. It is intended that the works at Acaderny-street will be kept fully employed as the space vacated by the men sent to the new Foundry will be at once utilise l for much needed extensions in the other departments of the manufacture. Work will now be done in Warrington which of necessity has hitherto been done in the other works of the Company and the increased facilities for production will in time undoubtedly lead to the employment of many more workmen and the new Foundary will be p. e-eminent as one of the latest examp!es in this country of modern foundry equipment—well able to meet, for a long time to come, the growing competition in this rnportant bra nch of industry.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Mrs. J. R. Buckton.

We regret to record the death of Mrs. J. R. Buckton (widow of the late Mr. James Rhoades Buckton, solicitor, Warrington), which took place at her resi- dence at Wbitchurch on Friday of last week. The deceased lady, who was 77. years of age, formerly resided at Warrington, and took an active interest in church work in connection with the Parish church and missions and also in St. Peter's parish, and did good service by her connection with the Ladies' Charity, the Clergy Daughters' School, and the Ladies' School, now Smith-street School. After her husband's death she took up her residence at Whitchurch, where she also engaged in church work as long as her health permitted, and where, as at Warrington, she was deeply respected. She leaves two sons (of whom Colonel Buckton is one) and two daughters to mourn her loss. The remains of the deceased lady were interred in the Cemetery at Warrington on Monday afternoon, amid many manifestations of respect and esteem.

WARRINGTON LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the General Council of the Warrington Liberal Association was held at the Liberal Club on Wednesday evening, Mr. C. E. Parker presiding over an exceptionally large attendance. The following officers were elected : —President, Mr. C. E. Parker ; vice-presidents, Messrs. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., Frank A. Roberts, Ziba Armitage, Edwin J, Guest, Alderman Pierpoint, Henry Roberts, Alderman Tinnion, and William Madden ; treasurer, Mr. William Furness ; secretary, Mr. J. T. Locker. The annual meeting of the Executive Committee was afterwards held, Councillor James Evans pre- siding. The Committee re-elected Councillor Evans as chairman, and Councillor Wilkinson as deputy- chairman. _

The forthcoming Board of Guardians elections were discussed, and it was decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Ward Committees.

STATE OF CRIME IN THE BOROUGH.

-- Chief Constable's Annual Report

The Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) has just issued his annual report to the Watch Committee, from which it appears that Warrington still continess in its immunity from serious crime, the year 1906 being as free in this respect as any which preceded it. Under the head of indictable offences 123 crimes were com- mitted as against 97 in 1905, but of these the great majority-95—consisted of petty larceny. Three burglaries only were reported to the police who succeeded in one case in tracing and arresting the cul- prit. It is an old saying that there would be no thieves if there were no receivers and whilst persons of this ilk are difficult to trace the police during the year brought about the conviction of four out of five pro- ceeded against. Turning to minor offences such as drunnesneess, &c., the number of persona proceeded against shows a substantial decrease as compared with the previous year. In his report the CHIEF CONSTABLE points out that the authorized strength of the police force is 73 and that in addition to the central offices there are four out stations. Seventy-one men have passed an Ambulance Examination and hold certificates, while several are in possession of the coveted medallions of the St. John Ambulance Brigade. First aid has been rendered to 39 injured persons during the year, some of the cases being of a very grave nature. The utility of the horse ambulance ia demonstrated by the fact that ho fewer than 49 calls were made upon it during 1906, as against 37 in the previous year. During the year the police made 24 arrests (several being particularly smart captures) of criminals wanted for various offences by the Police of other towns, and have succeeded in recovering (irrespective of stolen articles) lost property of the aggregate value of £B6 ; the various articles have been restored to their respective owners. The cost of the Police, in pay, clothing and equip- ment, in 1906 has amounted to £6,947 us. id., as com- pared with £6,626 16s. 4d. in the preceding year. This increase is accounted for by the augmentation of the Force by four men. The Police Superannuation Fund is in an exceedingly satisfactory condition, amounting at the year's end to £15,557 10s. 7d., the income for the year being Zl,OlB 3s. 9d., and the disbursements, 18s. 7d. dn the pension list are now-1 ex-Chief Inspector,2 ex-Sergeants, and 2 ex-Constables.

CHRISTMAS DAY, 1906,

It was certainly a day of surprises. Them were presents discovered in the most out-of-the- way places, but, above ail, the drawing-room door was locked, and was not opened untli we— my mother, my two brothers, and myself—were assembled outside it. At last we crowded alto the room, which was literally covered with strangely shaped parcels, which, When opened, were found to contain soldiers, games, books, chocolates, and many other longed-for articles. It was now about nine o'clock, so we all trooped in to a really very jolly breakfast. The morning was spent (and seetted to go Much too quickly) in re-examining our numer- ous presents Of course, Poll, the parrot, re- ceived his .Christmas stocking, but not seeming to appreciate it much, Gerald, my brother, asked, "Don't you want it, Polly?" The answer came very suddenly, for, with a positive shudder, Poll dzepped it and said, or, rather, wailed., "No—o." We had lunch at about half-past une, alter whilir we prepared to spend a quiet afternoon reading, etc. But this we were not destined to dtr, for three young lady friends called, to liven -us up, they said, which, indeed, they did, and there were shrieks af laughter caused by the contents of musical (?), paper hat, or firework trackers. One young lady, after tea., tfoceeded to solemnly tie round her chin a 'tax paper bon- net. The next moment we wrr,:., z.startled to hear the loud crying of a baby, taAoni, however, we could not s.e. Presently t•..;e noise stopped, and.the lady in the bonset said, "It was I; I'm the baby," ay *Le Uld out one of the nittAical (Ill?) instruments found in the crackers.

At about five they left us, and half-an-nour afterwards, dinner, consisting of the proverbial turkey and plum-pudding, followed by "a jolly good tuck-in," as one of the boys said, of nuts, fruit, etc., was served.

After dinner we all sang hymns, which were interrupted by the ting-ting and clip-clip of tambourines and castanets, and, going to the door, we saw about a dozen niggers singing a Christmas carol to that funny accompaniment. At half-past nine we were all in bed, and so finished one of the merriest Christmas Days I have ever known. L. G. F.-

MY DEAR NIECES AND NEPHEWS,--•

• I expect there will be many eager &Yea glancing through the " Corner " this week, to see who has been fortunate enough to win the "How I spent Ohristmas-day" prizes. I have had quite a big .task in sorting; out all the contributions. As I intend publishing the best ten or twelve, this means that I shall have to devote part of next week's "Corner" for the few that remain over from this week. I said in another Letter, I shall give prizes o the three best, but I am not telling you this week who are the lucky members. I think it best to let you read through them yourselves first, and try and guess who you imagine are the victors. Running this competition two weeks prohibits me settling the Picture Post- card Prize next week, as I hoped to have done, so I must ask you all to look out for the follow- ing week. lam still receiving pretty cards every meek, but I would like to see them roll in a bit faster. Of course, there is nothing to prevent any member sending in more than one, if they wish. Well, I must not be selfish, and take up any more valuable space, whioh this week really belongs to my relatives. So please receive best wishes for your welfare from

UNCLE NED.

HOW I SPENT CHRISTMAS DAY.

When we came down for breakfast tin he morning, we all wished each other a "Happy Christmas," after which we went to church. When we got back from church, one of my sistera had arrived who was not able to come before, and now we made a complete family party. We had dinner at two o'clock, which consisted of a fine large turkey, vegetables-, sausages, arid sauce, for the first course; plum pudding and sauce for the second course, after which we had dessert. After dinner we each gave our presents away, and I think I was very fortunate in having so many nice ones. The afternoon was gone by the time we had viven our presents, and laughed and chatted over them. In the evening we played different games, such as H4apla, Society Misfitz. etc. Afterwards we had supper, and then went to bed, after having a very enjoyable day.

MARGARET MARY PRESS

HOW I SPENT CHRISTMAS

On Christmas Eve I had a present from my aunt; it was a nice muslin pinafore. I had also a fancy pocket handkerchief from my sister. My mother gave me a pretty brooch in the shape of a daisy, and also a blue woollen. scarf. On Christmai3 morning I had eevera/ picture postcards and a Christmas card. I went to church by eleven o'clock in the morn- ing. When I came home I enjoyed myself very much. On Boxing Day I went to my friend's to tea and supper. I now think I will cloee, hoping I have become one of your members of the Children's League.

OLIVE MAY KIVELL.

HOW I SPENT CHRISTMAS DAY.

First thing I did was to have my breakfast. After breakfast I went for a. walk, and when I returned Santa Claus gave me a doll in a box, end gave me a Christmas handkerchief, too. Then next came dinner-time, and I had for it roast beef and: plum-pudding. I enjoyed it. very much. After dinner I and my sister dressed the Chriatmse-tree. We put on it a box of chocolates, a glass ball, and 8, great many more things. Then it was time for tea. We had for our tea a cake, which mother made, and some barns. After tea we had apples, oranges, nuts, and sweet*. Theo came supper. I had for it two mince-pies; then it was time to go to bed. I was very sorry then. I think I will close my letter now.

DORIS THACKER.

HOW I SPENT CHRISTMAS DAY,

I was awakened on Christmas morning by the carol singers, but the carols did not interest me. I was too busy wondering what was in my stocking. As soon as it was light I was out of bed to see what I had got. After breakfast, my brother and eister and I played with our toys until we heard a knock at the door, and went to s•ee who was there. The postman had brought us a big parcel right from Kidderminster. You should have heard the noise we made when mother was cutting tho strings; we all wanted to k-now what was inside. I received a few cards from some friends. Dinner-time came, finding us all very hungry. We played games until a few friends came to tea. After tea, we all went into the room. There mother had decked a Christmas tree up with all sorts of pretty things. In a while father put some fairy lights; they looked very pretty, with sparks flying In all directions. They were a certain kind which would not burn anything. z'ozne of us sang, and recited, and we had a very pleasant time. It was soon bedtime, and was very tired, bat happy.

ALICE EBURY.

HOW I SPENT MY CHRISTMAS DAY.

I awoke early in the morning to await the tv.stLnan's knock, and then I went down to look at my numerous presents, for I had a good many, as my birthday was on Christmas Dv, and to examine the pretty carde. I went to church in the morning, and it looked very pretty indeed, the holly berries 'midst the ever- greens.

...4ftdi church I went home to enjoy roast duck and plum pudding. After I had had my dinner I went for a nice long walk, an.d came home to tea. Mother let me cut up my birthday cake.

In the evening we all sat around the fire, and asked each other riddles, and, Uncle Ned, a wonderful "Christulasy" feeling came over me as the firelight danced on the holly berries on the wall, and the wood crackled in the fire, and I was reading at the same time Sir Walter Scott's poem, "Christmas in the Olden Time," about the boar's head decked with bays and rosemary, and how delightfully they greeted Christmastide years and years ago. I went to bed thinking that I had spent a. most happy Christmas Day, and I hope that you, Uncle Ned, spent the same.

So, hoping that I am not too late for the competition, and trusting that my letter will meet with your approval, ELSIE LEWIS.

VAUDEVILLE ENTERTAINMENT AT

THE PARR HALL.

Variety entertainments on the two-shows-a-night principle appeal strongly to the general, public and Warringtonians have this week shown their appreciation of a programme of this character provided by Mr. H. E. Barnes (who is associated with the new theatre scheme) in the Parr Hall, each performance having attracted a crowded audience. First class talent is introduced in an all-round strong company and the programme also has the great merit of excluding all that might offend even the most particular taste. The three Boccacios are perhaps the most successful of the artistes and they Combine with excellent musical ability humour—grotesque but none the less irresistible, their efforts keeping the audience in roars of laughter. Included in the trio is Francia Boccacio, who can claim to be ranked amongst the foremost of the world's mandolinists. In intricate classical selections he dis- plays a masterly command over his instrument and a full appreciation of the possibilities of the various compositions. The Bensons make their first bow to a Warrington audience and right heartily are they welcomed. As harmonious duettists and dancers they occupy a leading position whilst they also contribute largely to the humour of the evening. Mr. D. W. Watson "The world's greatest one-armed cornettist and post-born soloist " provides a somewhat unique item and no trace of his physical disability is discernible in his playing. A pretty sketch is presented by the two Kays, vocalists of considorable talent, whilst Miss Ivy Nilson and Miss Violet Nunn achieve great success. Thornbury, a lightning cartoonist and ventriloquist, works with surprising rapidity particularly when with a few deft strokes he trant•forms a sea scene into a land- scape, whilst not the least effective of his works is a picture in smoke. Sinclair and Parr create much amusement with their patter whilst they rank high as vocalists. An excellent entertainment is brought to a close by a brief cinematograph exhibition depicting the adventures of an inexperienced chauffeur in Paris. The instrumental accompaniments are provided by a capable orchestra under the leaderAip of Mr. W. A. Guest which also supplies some excellent selections. The per- formances will be repeated to-night at 7 and 9 and we would advise our readers who have not already dons so, to pay a visit to the 114irr Hall.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 2, 1907. THE FREE CHURCHES.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
(By Our London Correspondent.)

NATIONAL rnmE CHURCH COUNCIL-

Great interest is being shown 'among Free Churchmen in the annual meetings of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches, which are to be held next week in ,Leeds. Most careful arrangements have been made, and everything points to unusually im- _ .

portant gatherings. 7-S'-ex- t Sunday the mee-tin,,.3 will start with services in the Coliseum, Lecis, conducted by the Rev. John McNeill, the well- known Evangelist. .

===== 0002601_19070302_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
SPECIAL FEATURES.

It is difficult to say What are the outstanding features of the programme to be put before the National Council. To take first the de- votional element: there are a number of ser- raons by some of the most eloquent, preachers among the Free Churches. As is usual, the Tuesday morning session starts with a sermon by the retiring President. Mr. Scott Lidgett has worked splendidly during his year of office, and the Council will not fail to show its appre- ciation of his services. The Council Sermon, which is always such a feature of the gather- ings, will this year be preached by the Rev. J. H. Jowett, M.A. This ought to be one of the most notable items of a notable pro- gramme. The sermon on Wednesday is by the Re;. Charles Brown, and that on Thursday by Rev. W. L. Watkinson, whilst the .Devotional Session on Thursday afternoon is being taken by Mr. C. F. Aked, Dr. Brook, and Rev. J. D. Jones, M.A., B.D. In addition to this, some eight preaching services have been arranged for Wednesday evening, as well as a special Welsh sermon by the Rev. H. Elvet Lewis, M.A-

PUBLIC QUESTIONS.

Turning to some of the public questions Which will engage the attention of the Coun- cil, Education comes first, and a :weighty pro- nouncement ought to be made. Temperance policy, Old Age Pensions' and the Congo Ques- tion are being taken on Wednesday morning; while on Thursday morning resolutions on tne Hague Conference and Opium will be con- sidered.

SOME INTERESTING SUBJECTS

There are several papers which should arouse keen interest, and possibly discussion. Sir Oliver Lodge's Paper on "The Reformed Church as an Engine •of Progress" will be given On Tuesday afternoon. Another impor- tant subject to be dealt with is -." The Holy Spirit. in Scripture and Experience." Dr. Horton speaks to this, and he is followed in the same .(Wednesday morning) session by. an address on the "Decline of Home Life,' by Rev. J. G. Greenhough, M.A. Two important papers will be read on Wednesday afternoon on "The Public Ministry of Women," by. Mrs. Rendel Harris, and "-Christianity in Relation to Modern Social and Intellectual Problems," by Rev. Thomas Phillips, B.A. Mr. Phillips is well able to speak on this latter subject., mixing as he does with the teeming life of the Metropolis in his institutional work at Blooms- bury. Another live subject is that put down for Thursday morning, when Professor Peake deals with the "OhlTestament in the Relig- ions Life of To-day " • while equally interesting in another direction is Dr. Nicoll's paper on "The Ritual Commission and the Duty of the Free Churches."

===== 0002601_19070302_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW PRESIDENT

Dr. Rendel Harris, the new President of the National Council, is a man of most scholarly distinction. He is a member- of the Society df Friends—the first Quaker to assnme the l'residency of the National Council. Ai rim account of his interesting career, from the pen of the Rev.' J. M. G. Owen appears in this month's Free Church Chronicle. 'lbis issue, by the way, is a special Leeds number, contain- ing all information about the meal igs, and is profusely -illustrated.

FEDERATION REPORT.

An important session in conneticn with the National Conneil.is that on Tuesday after- noon, when the Secretary's Report is pre- sented. I have been able to .e(.2 an advance copy of this interesting document. Mr. Law refers to the past twelve months of a year of steady progress. "It seems difficult," he says, "to realise that the work is of so recent a growth, such is the permanence and stability it has now reached. If a sign of Hie is the creation of off-shoots, our movement can claim to be of abundant vitality, for its branches now go in all directions there seems to be hardly any phase of national life with which our work does not come into contact. The social and missionary work—to name only two recent departures--contain in themselves abundant scope for continuous service."

SPIIIITUAL WCRI:

Great stress is laid upon the spiritual work of the Free .Church Council. An account is given of the Free Church Keswick" started last year, as well as numerous district Conven- tions held by local Federations and Councils. The numerous admirers of Gipsy Smith, the National Council's Evang,clist, will read with pleasure of the wondeffill •success that has been accorded to his mission in the -United States, of which full accounts are given. Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, MA., as President of the Council, has, di:rity: the year, paid visits to the Federations, and records his impressions of the deep hold the Free Church Council ideals and work have taken, upon • Free Churchmen throughout the country.

THE NATIONAL COUNCIL AND EDUCATION.

The Report deals at some length with the action of the Council occasioned by the intro- duction of the Education Bill in Parliament, and its pregress through the House of Lords. :.z2eeiai and frequent meeting of the Education Committee were held, the various clauses of the Bill being considered with special reference to their bearing upon Free Church principles. These, deliberations cu;minated in the sum- moning of the whole Conn:-il for a special ses- sion to review the situation.

VARIED WORE.

The Report shows that the Federation move- ment opens the door to much valuable work in municipal and civic life, and that local governing bodies and other public authorities are turnvg more and more to the Free Church Councils to carry out Nonconformist religious services, appoint chaplains, etc., in Workhouse asylums and other public institutions. Woman's work and influence in the Councils finds expression in that collateral organisa- tion of the Nation Council, the Free Church Girls' Guild.; through whose efforts much last- ing goods is being effected upon the lives and characters of the girlhood of to-day. There are now 13 permanent hoinc; in active work and there is an employment bureau estab-, fished for the benefit of the girls.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====

BARROW v. WIDNES.

At Barrow. The home team commenced operations against a fairly strong wind, and Widnes at once attacked, following a smart break-away by Lally, but Gifford very cleverly gathered the ball when surrounded by the Widnes men on his own line, and transferred to the centre, where Barrow forged ahead, and Wharton scored ten minutes from the start. The kick at goal failed. Twice in quick succession Barrow were penal- ised, and Davies kicked two goals from the places. Half-time was called with the score :—Widnee, 2 goals (4 points), Barrow 1 try (3 points). In the second half Barrow soon registered a try by Fox,, which Wharton failed to improve. With the wind behind them Barrow ran up a succession of points, the Widnes men being penned .in their own quarters. Tries were scored by Fox (2), Brough, Hughes, Jardine, Mellor, and Burns. Mellor and Gifford scored a goal each, but before no-side was called Mellor and Bowers were injured, and Barrow were playing four forwards only at the finish. Result :—Barrow 2 goals 8 tries (28 points),Widnes 2 goals (4 points). Teams : Barrow—Gifford, back; V. Woodburn, E. Bracktiank, W. Mellor, and J. B. Fox, three-quarter backs ; J. D. Wharton and J. P. Barnes, half backs ; W. Brough, T. Hughes, J. Bowers, J. Jardine, A. Hannah and C. Brown, forwards.

Widnes—Jones, back; Davies, McCarthy, Lambert, and Taylor, three-quarter backs; Lally and Kennedy, half backs; Argent, Cochrane, Rigby Turner, Carr, and Lloyd, forwards. Referee, Mr. Cross, Wigan. RUNCORN v. ST. HELENS. At Runcorn before 1,500 spectators. Immediately after the start, Jolley got possession from the pack, and passed to Deakin, who raced over the line, and scored, Jolley adding a goal. Hardly had play resumed when Deakin broke away with a fine run, and passed to Butter- worth, the latter player getting over. Jolley failed with the goal kick. A mistake by Jolley gave the visitors a footing in the home half,but this was neutral- ised by the Runcorn backs, play settling in the St. Helens half. M. Creevey and Toole relieved, but Jolley again handed the bill to Dixon, who added a further try, Jolley failing at goal. Resuming, St. Helens attacked, and Toole breaking away, passed to C. Creevey, who scored at the corner. J. Creevey landed a fine goal from the touch line. St. Helens continued to press up to half time, when the score Nies—Runcorn 1 goal and 3 tries (11 points), St. Helens 1 goal ad I try (5 points). After the interval play settled in the home half, where J. Creevey, getting possession, scored after a good effort. The same player failed to improve. Toole, Atkinson, and C. Creevey transferred play to the Runcorn end, but Jolley relieved, and after a scramble in the visitors half, Flannagan smartly gathered the ball and scored near the posts, Jolley failing at goal. After a temporary injury to Watton, St. Helens attacked strongly, but without success. Result :—Runcorn 1 goal and 4 tries (14 points), St. Helens 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points). Teams : Runcorn—Harrison, back; Deakin, Butterworth, Moran, and Flanagan, three-quarter backs; Jolley and Watton, half-backs; Walker, Padbury, Richardson, Dixon, Kennedy, and Macdonald, forwards. St. Heleus—Barton, back; C. Creevey, Billen, Atkin- son, and J. Creevey, three-quarters backs; M. Creevey, and Toole, hal f-backs ; Briers, Mooney, Maverty, Smith, Whiteley and Pope, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Asquith, Wakefield. Touch judges, Messrs. A.Jones, Warrington, and H. Davies, Flixton.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

--- ---- - - ------- - Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
Stockton Heath Parish Council.

Sir,—Will you permit me to remind my fellow parishioners and ratepayers, of the parish meeting on Monday evening next? At this meeting the election of Parish Councillors for the next three years will take place, and it is essential that everyone who takes an interest—and who does not ?—in our local government should be present in order to ensure that the fittest and best persons are elected to look after the welfare of this important and growing community. Our parish is rapidly ripening into a comely and valuable plum, very tempting and well worth appropriation. It behoves us all as ratepayers to see that our Council is constituted of men who will " hold our end up" and take care that whenever such appropriation is attempted it shall not succeed unless due regard be paid and no damage done to the tree which has borne and produced such goodly fruit.—Yours &c., H. J. WESTBROOK. Stockton Heath, 28th February, 1907.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
A Veteran's Thanks.

Sir,—My old comrades of half a century ago, and my- self would like with your permission to express in your columns our humble and heartfelt thanks for the kindly and cordial way in which we were treated during the recent unveiling of the War Memorial and the attendant festivities. Having passed the last twenty years and more in Warrington I have witnessed the thoroughness and the wholeheartedness with which the good old town esters into the spirit of any similar occasion. Whether it be a Royal Jubilee! An incorporative commemora- tion! A "send off" to the war! A Coronation festival ! A home coming of "all that was left of them " Warringtonians vie with each other in making each one an unequalled success. Wherever we went or whoever we met we received the greatest kindness and the best of good wishes. The dinner at Parr Hall on Friday was truly a most enjoyable occasion "without stint," "without msasure and as one of our old boys tersely remarked" Eh lads! what a day we're havin." Every man in our group had passed the allotted span, several of us verging close on fourscore, and I need not say how deeply touched one and all of us were by the all round kindness shown us. We would like to acknowledge our special obligations to his Worship (Alderman James Smethurst) Warrington's genial, übiquitous and hard working Mayor, to Mr. Henry Roberts, the patriotic Chairman of the Memorial Committee and the large-hearted gentlemen composing it for their goodness in haying invited us to meet and speak to that illustrious soldier Sir Redvers Buller. Also we thank Mr. Inspector Phillips and those of his men who looked after our comfort—not omitting those gentlemen of the press who gave us kindly thought and notice. Believe me, Sir, the proceedings of Thursday and Friday last will live as a bright and happy memory in the heart of every soldier fortunate enough to have been present, while in none will it remain happier or brighter than in the gladdened hearts of the "Boys of the Old Brigade."

H.W.H.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
The Election of Guardians.

Sir,—lt is a long time since I have seen a letter in your columns so full of bigotry, intolerance, and unjustifiable insinuations as that from Mr. W. Dawkins. Is it not a good illustration of what Mr. C. E. Parker has pointed out, that a section of the temperance party may do their cause more harm than good by intemperate Opposition to the trade, and I may add by the unreason- able and ridiculous assumption that those who are not total abstainers cannot properly belong to the Temper- ance party. Mr. Dawkins has given absolutely no evidence that Mesers. Davies, Payton, and W. C. Brooks have acted, or are likely to act with less impartiality and disinterestedness than other Guardians, and in the absence of such evidence, his remarks can only be characterised as biased and prejudiced. Most people will admit that total abstainers do a great deal of good by their example, but when they arrogate to themselves all the other virtues, and deny by implication that people who are engaged in the trade, or those who are not total abstainers cannot have an equal claim to other virtues, they are simply putting themselves out of court. Mr. Dawkins' remarks about, agents of insurance com- panies not being suitable are like his other insinuations, absolutely devOid of substance. He is evidently a total abstainer with regard to the use of facts. As to Mr. Ashcroft, I hope the ratepayers will remember how boldly he opposed the Workhouse Extension Scheme, and thereby helped very materially to defeat it. For this alone he is entitled to their hearty and continued support. It is on the question of economy and the Reform of the Poor Law that ratepayers should seriously consider how their votes should be given. Do they think that two-thirds ot three-fourths of the poor rate should be spent in administration expenses? Should any nation which claims to be enlightened and progressive continue such a system ? It is a system which violates every sound principle of political economy. Con- trary alike to common sense, common Justice and common feelings of humanity. Centralisation with respect to Poor Law is an absolute failure. The only hope is by a return to constitutional Principles by throwing the duty and responsibility upon each locality of adopting a comprehensive system of outdoor relief. About nine tenths of the labour of Parliament consists of undoing what has been done before and done badly, and many of our so-called reforms are merely going hack to the wisdom of our forefathers. This will be the case sooner or later with the Poor Law as I cannot believe that ratepayers will permanently submit to a heavy burden and probably an increasing burden, mainly for the benefit of officials and professional tramps. It is a doubtful point whether the deserving and respectable poor get ten per cent. of the Poor Rate, and if they get that much it is accom- panied by degradation and suffering which is a disgrace to our boasted civilisation. I suppose if footballing and cricketing were interfered with or taxed in any way, there would be revolution in a week, but nothing else is considered of sufficient importance to rouse the spirit of the present generation. The large proportion of the poor rate which for many years has been practi- cally.wasted would go a long way towards an Old Age Pension i Fu Parliamentnd. Whatever Parliament may have done or sanctionedd n the past is not in my opinion,worth con- sidering. If we admit for the sake of argument that. the Poor Law was in some respects a well meant experi- ment we should be equally ready to admit that the administration of it is a public scandal, and that there is no hope whatever of the present system being a success either economically or from a humanitarian point of view. I hope in conclusion, if any of our readers can show that my views are unsound, they will doso,as criticism and opposition are always more bene- ficial tosociety than apathy, but if they cannot I hope they will vote for those guardians.who will,if it is only experimentally, adopt, with proper safe+mards a better system of outdoor relief.—yours, &c., 9 679, Knutsford-road, Latchford.

W. CHRIMES.

-- Sir,—The time will soon be here when all who take an nterest in the town can show by their actions that they desire men to sit in judgment over the poor who are not allied with the drink. It seems incongruous for a publican to take the last few coppers from a poor man and then pity his wife and children because they have no bread. I really do not think it is a position in touch with their sympathies or they would help to remove the cause by separating themselves from a trade which does a good share in creating the poverty and degradation so prevalent in our midst. The publican—if he is a sensitive man at all—must have gruesoine sights enough in the discharge of his daily duties without being allowed to sit as a Guardian and see the pitiful sights and hear the heart-rending tales of distress from the women who come before them. It is with a feeling of duty that I appeal to the men wh,p are not allied with the drink trade to arouse themselves and relieve the publicans from the work of their own hands,enlist all the powers which will tend to bring forth that which is pure and righteous. Let those men and women who in private circles or at home are capable of saying who ought or who ought not to rule, go amongst the people and enlighten them—for alas, too many of them do not know how to vote for their own interest. Then on the election day go forth in good numbers to work in the wards which are being contested, and woe be to those who use bribery and corruption. slay no man give his vote for the promise of a drink either on the election day or any other tittle. This is not a matter of politics and ought not to be fought on those lines. Let a higher standard of ethics move you to go forth with an unbiassed mind and a determination that right shall prevail.

AGNES SINIPSON. Bewsey-road, February 2Gtb, 1907.
