MESSRS. GARNETT'S NEW WORKS.

OPENING BY MRS. GARNETT. ---- Employees Entertained.

Saturday marked an important epoch in the history of Messrs. Hebert Garnett and Sons, Warrington, for it witnessed the opening of the handsome new works by Mrs. Garnett. The firm celebrated the occasion by inviting the employees, their wives and sweethearts, and a few friends, to tea, and there was an assembly numbering about 250 in one of the large new workshops. The room had been very tastefully decorated and presented a bright and cheerful appearance. On the walls were portraits of the late Mr. Robert Garnett, who founded the firm in 1524, the late Mr. Robert Garnett, of Hall Nook, Penketh,Mr. David Garnett and Mr.J.R Gray.On I arriving the guests were received by Mr. and Mrs. R.E. Garnett and a cordial welcome having been. extended ! to them, proceeded to the tea room. Here a very I happy gathering was presided over by Mr. R. E. ! Garnett and amongst others present were : , Mrs. R. Garnett, Mr. and Mrs. P. C. Garnett, Mr. and Mrs.D. Garnett, Mr. and Mrs.Jenks, Mr. Chandley,Mr. and Mrs.R.H. Posnett, Mr.and Mrs. Charles E. Parker, Mr. and Mrs. John Garnett, Mr.and Mrs. Withenshaw, Mr. and Mrs. Christopher Bradshaw, Mr. and Mrs. , Cathcart-Smith, Mrs.Doerz, Miss Coop, Mr. Riley, Mr. I Birtles,Mr. T. E. Eccles, (Messrs. Woolfall and Eccles I architects,Liverpool), Mr.Dolan (builder).The following i committee had charge of the arrangements : —Messrs.: P. C. Garnett, D. R. Hodge, P. II Brown, A. White, I and J. &mon. An excellent tea was served, the catering being in the hands of Atkinson's Gads, Ltd. Afterwards there was some interesting speech-making. Mr. DAVID Giro:Err said he was asked to perform a very simply day, and that was to invite Mrs. Robert Garnett to formally open their new works. A gathering of that kina brought back to memory things long past and gone. He could remember well a social gathering perhaps a fourth the size of the present one and which- was hold in the old works at Penketh about forty-five years ago. He was thankful that to-night there was a reunion not only of hands but also of hearts in connection with their firm, both now and for the future. A gathering like that should serve the purpose of instilling into the minds of those present the value of working in unity for the further- ance and accomplishment of good work, with high aims and the determination that they would not be secend to any place in Lancashire or in the whole of the United Kingdom. (Applause.) He thought he was justified in saying from past experience that the firm of Robert Garnett and Sons had sent out furniture equal to anything that had ever been produced in this country. About 40 or 50 years ago they passed through what might be described as a barbarous age in the designing of furniture. Now they were reviving to an extent some of the old traditions and they were not too proud to copy their forefathers,such as Sheraton, Chippendale, and Hepplewhite. Having in mind past associations, it was a special pleasure to he asked to meet the employees on this occasion.He did not want to touch upon politics or the Independent Labour Party or the head of the 1.L.P., but he would say to his young friends present that if they had not read Longfellow's ' Psalm of Life" they should do so to-morrow and make up their minds not to be like dumb-driven cattle bet " Be ye heroes of the strife." (Applause.) The spirit of the young man was to a large extent being crushed out of him by those who ought to know better.He believed, however,that at these works they had a loyal set of men who would sup- port his nephew in carrying on the business. That night they held in joyful memory two who had passed away but who were particularly dear to their hearts—he referred to his honoured father and dear brother. (Applau/re). Mrs. ROBERT GARNETT said it gave her great pleasure to be amongst them and to see so many gathered together, and also to be surrounded by so many members of her own family. She was glad to hear Mr. David Garnett say they had joyful memories of those who had passed away. She had long since learnt to call tears idle tears ; their memories were joyful and their examples were the greatest possession of those who remained. It was a delightful thing to know that from its foundation in 1824 that firm had been noted for the integrity of its work and its honest toil. They who were toiling in succession would, she hoped, be enabled to continue that reputation. She hoped that firm did not exist merely to make money, but that they were looking forward with Rudyard Kipling to the time when No man shall work for money No man shall work for fame ; But all for the joy of working. Mrs. Garnett proceeded to recall the names of old work- men, including John Cotton Henry Booth, Andrew Crighton and Robert Campbell, and made sympathetic references to them. Continuing she said she had watched the rise of those works with very great interest. She used to come down when they were digging the foundations ; being somewhat of an antiquarian she liked to look into the soil and inquire about it. She found that a fratery had long since stood on the site and the old friars had walked about in green fields and gone down to the river where clear waters supplied them with fish for their dinner. Then when the fratery bad gone, a Grammer School, another seat of learning, came. Now that firm had taken up the place and their efforts would not he less noble than those of the schools of learning to which she had referred, if they did good work. She had great pleasure in pronouncing the works open and she mentioned with gratitude the fact that during their erection no serious accident had occurred, so that the buildings had been completed without any trouble or sorrow. (Applause). Mr. R. E. GARNETT then delivered a long and interesting address, in the course of which he said he took their warm welcome as an indication of the pleasure which the presence of his wife afforded. (Applause.) He saw no reason why they should not be proud of that occasion and of one another. (Hear, hear.) He trusted there was permeating every depart- ment of that business a feeling of mutual respect and that it would remain as long as the firm stood. (Applause.) He wanted to feel that the workmen had confidence in the firm and to know that the firm had confidence in them. (Applause.) Mr. Garnett expressed great pleasure at the presence of their architect, Mr. Eccles, who, he said, was the friend of all who did good work. Through many difficulties in the erection of the works Mr. Eccles had guided them with consummate skill. They were sorry that Mr. Woolfall (Mr. Eccles' partner), who had taken great interest in the project, could not be with them. They wished publicly to express their thanks to the builder, !qr. Dolan. and the men working under him for the good and economical work they had done. The foundations of the business were laid deep and true in 1824 by a man whose whole aim in life was to stimulate his workmen to do the best that hands could produce. The business was sustained by his (the speaker's) father; Mr. David Garnett had put in as solid work as anyone; and it was also sustained by Mr. Gray. When the Penketh works were burnt down in 1901, it necessitated their looking for fresh accommodation and as the old works of Niessrs.Fletcber,Russell & Co., were empty they took them on a lease. They found that by having the works in closer contact with their show rooms and office staff, they worked more economically and success- fully than at Penketh, and the result was that the firm began to consider whether it was not possible to have their works concentrated in such a form that they could Practically have the whole of the business con- ducted under one roof. They decided upon the erection of the new works the completion of which they were celebratine, that night. Mr. KINGDON, in returning thanks,said he appreciatad the compliment more from the point of view of the firm with which he was connected than as a personal honour. He believed that in the years to come Chambers of Commerce would be a greater power in the land than they were now. Whatever they could bring against the present Goverment, he thought they must admit that it was more commercially-inclined than any Government they ever had. He was certain of one thins; he did not think they ever had such a good President of the Board of Trade as Mr. Lloyd-George. There was a marked change in the way in which deputa- tions were received, for instead of everything they suggested being criticised and difficulties pat in the way, the department said: "Assuming we can improve matters, tell us what you want, and we will see if you can have it." The Board of Trade had recently appointed an Advisory Committee to help it in its deliberations and the Committee was recruited almost entirely from the Chambers of Commerce. The Government were recognizing with the Board of Trade that the way in which to keep up their communications with the com- mercial community was through the larger and more important Chambers of Commerce._ ___

The Examiner, Saturday, March 2, 1907.

by paying a tribute to the work of Mr.Jenks and of all associated with the launching of the new works. (Applause.) Hearty cheers were given for Mr. and Mrs. R. E. Garnett and for Mrs. Robert Garnett.

Pritchard and R. Barlow, a Lancashire dialect reading by Mr. R. Irons, a duet by Messrs. Cook and Bailey, 'cello solo by Mr. F. Cousen and humorous songs by Messrs. J. Emson and R. Harper. The proceedings closed with a dAnce, an elcient band being in attendance.

DESCRIPTION OF THE WORKS.

The new factory is a six storeyed building, 133ft.long and 301 t. wide, the floors varying in height from 12ft. b'in. to Oft. The latest possible development for Ihbour saving has been eagerly sought after and adopted and every machine for the cheapening of labour intro- duced. Efficiency is further enhanced by automatic suction of all refuse from the machines and workmen's benches direct to the boiler furnace. Throughout, the building has been fitted with a sprinkler installation, so that in the event of fire, a 7,000 gallon tank at the top of the tower would be immediately drawn upon to deal with the outbreak at the commencement, aud at the same time the fire alarm would ring as long as tbe sprinklers were operating. But the chief factor of con- centration lies here, that practically the showrooms and workshops are under one roof, the works being separated from the showrooms only by a fireproof compartment, which has been used as a tower space to carry the tank. Immediately between the works and the showrooms a lift is constantly running throughout the day. Power is obtained from a large gas engine, supplied by the National Company, which, aided by suction gas, is the cheapest power known. The engine is of sufficient capacity to drive in addition the dynamo whereby electricity is generated for 450 lights in the works and showrooms. Whilst adhering to the old form of season- ing, the latest development in timber drying has been introduced. &cross the spacious yard lie the cart sheds, lining out sheds, the paint shops, and workmen's dining room, contiguous to which are the stables and care- taker's house, the whole forming as complete, compact, comprehensive and business-like arrangement as it is possible to conceive.

PERMANENT BENEFIT BUILDING

SOCIETY.

Improved Position.

The annnal meeting of the Warrington Permanent Benefit Building Society was held in Cairo-street schools on Thursday evening, Mr. W. J. Shaw presiding. There was a good attendance including Messrs. T. Critchley, John Cooper, S. W. Dallow, C. H. Hibbert,R. Shackleton, F. Timperley (directors), A. J. Willett (solicitor), W. H. Woodcock (one of the auditors), and A. H. Clive (secretary).

===== 0002601_19070302_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPORT.

In their fortieth annual report the directors stated that the nett profit on the year's working amounted to £876 19s. 3d., out of which the directors proposed to pay a dividend of 4 per cent. (free of income tax),which would amount to £689 9s. Id., leaving a balance of £lB7 10s. 2d., of which £lOO was carried to undivided profit, and the sum of £B7 10s. 2d. forward to next year's account. Three properties had been sold during the year, and a nett profit realised of £238 is. sd.,which had been carried to property suspense account, the total of this account now standing at £519 14s. id. The directors took this opportunity of pointing out the security of the Society as an investment for further deposits and hoped the present shareholders would do their best to take up further shares and to introduce new members. The retiring directors were Messrs. Thomas Critchley, Chas. H. Hibbert, and Joseph Potter, who, being eligiblo,offered themselves for re-election.

PRAISE .AND CRITICISM.

The CHAIRMAN said he was sure they must look upon the statement with pleasure, seeing that they bad had another successful year with a balance sufficient to enable them to declare a 4 per cent. dividend. He would like to draw attention to the fact that the amount on notice for withdrawals which wag' a glaring item when the present board came into office—standing at £2,873 4s.—had now been paid off. No doubt some of the members would wonder why a bonus had not been declared as last year, but he would point out that during the year they had had certain sales of property which had returned a profit to the society. This income had been lent out on mortgage of first class security and which would not be thrown upon their hands but would bring in a remuneration which would always secure the members. So recently as in January they advanced £5lO on mortgage, which showed that a bonus could have heed paid if the transaction had not taken place. During the two years they had lent on mortgage £2,720. (Applause.) The Society was now able to advance money instead of having,like four years ago, to go about begging for assistance. The whole of the property was in a better condition than it was when the Board took over the responsibilities. The Society was never in a sounder or firmer position that at the present time. He would only say with regard to the past that had one-tenth part of the care been paid to the management and the general welfare of the Society the shareholders would never have had 40 per cent, taken away from that which they had earned. Instead of being able to declare 4 per cent. dividend that night, they would probably have been paying 6 and 7 per cent. continuously. It was not, however, always well and wise to pay a large dividend. _ .

On th; rnotion-of Nix. PARTRIDGE, seconded by Mr. STANLEY WRIGHT, the report was adopted. everal questions were then asked and answered. Mr. FEtANK SHAW suggested that a small committee along with the auditors be appointed to go into the question of management to see if nothing could be done to curtail expenses, so that they would have better socurity. The CHAIRMAN thought such a request was iafra dig. Did not the shareholders place implicit confidence in the Board to act beneficially to everyone concerned ? Mr. SHAW thought the Secretary might collect from the Widnes properties and dispense with the services of the Widnes collector.

The CHAIRMAN : It is preposterous to expect the Secretary to do it for his present salary of £125. Mr. STANLEY WRIGHT said he believed the success of the Society was mainly due to the energy of the Secretary and he sugested that the meeting should recommend the Board to take into consideration an increase of his salary. Mr. WOODCOCK said he could guarantee that the position of the Society was extremely sound and with care, he had no doubt such a state of things would continue. It was impossible, with the property that was handed over to the Board a few years since, to do more than they were doing at the present time. A large amount of property valued at a very high figure in Widnes and Warrington, proved not to beof a very high class order. Considering the large amount of property the society held which was only in a very few instances of a class that would not appreciate in value, it was undesirable to do more than they were doing. He as auditor heartily approved of the transference of the balance of profit, which was derived from the sale of property, to a suspensory fund to be utilized in any emergency which might arise. ELECTIONS.

Messrs. T. Critchley, C. H. Hibbert and Joseph Potter were re-elected directors ; Mr. A. Cook-,treasurer; Mr. William Twiss, surveyor; Messrs. E. Bradshaw and W. H. Woodcock, auditors. A vote of thanks to the chairman concluded the meeting.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL. Important Conference.

A conference took place at the Town Hall yesterday morning, between the Corporation and traders of War- rington and the directors of the Ship Canal Company with reference to the powers sought by the company in the Bill which has been introduced in the House of Lords this Session and will come before a committee on Tuesday next. One important matter in dispute was settled—the company undertaking not to abstract any more water from the river than they are at present entitled to—but whilc a long discussion took place on other questions involved in the Bill no decision was arrived at, and the matters will, it is presumed, have to be fought out in the Committee of the House. It will be remembered that the company pro. pose to pump water from the lower to the higher sections of the canal and it is feared that the water which will flow into the river will become brackish and unfit for boiler purposes. Many of the large manufacturers of the town take their water from the river for use in boilers, and it is of vital importance to them tbat the water should be kept free from salt.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
FORTHCOMING ELECTIONS.

PROBABLE CONTESTS.

March will be a busy month electorally as the burgesses will be called upon to elect representatives on Boards of Guardians, County, Rural District and Parish Councils. For the first time in its history there is to be a contest for the election of a repre- sentative of the Winwick Division on the Lancashire County Council and Mr. J. W. Knowles, Winwick, the retiring Councillor,will have to fight hard to retain his seat, a strong opponent in the person of Mr. Robert Forshaw having been nominated. Both gentlemen are well-known in the division whilst Mr. Forshaw has it in his favour that he resides in probably the most thickly populated portion of the division. In Burtonwood he is a well tried and highly esteemed servant •of the public and has been a member of the Parish Council since its formation, hav- ing latterly acted as Chairman, and representing the village on the Warrington Rural District Council. In his election address which we publish in another column, he points, with reasonable pride, to the work he has already done as a public representative, and assures the electors that he will do his best to safe- guard their interests. He has handed in several nomination papers and in the first of these he is pro- posed by Mr. Alfred Brooks, seconded by Mr. J. Clare Ridgway and supported by Messrs. George Copple, T. Greenhalgh, J. Cawthorn°, Revs.H. I. Wadison,and W. Pollock-Hill,T.Birtles,J.Massey and W.Hodgkinson.Mr. Knowles is proposed by Mr. James Bennett, seconded by Mr. W. F. Newton and supported by Messrs. J. Critchley, Shadrach Sutton, George Walker, H. S. Page, E. Lunt, J. C. Harrington, W. Smith and W. Fryer. The election will take place on Thursday and the result will probably be declared at Padgate the same evening. The polling booths are at Sankey Schools, National Schools, Padgate, and Hollins Green Schools.

Parish Councils.

The annual parish meetings in the various villages will be held on Monday evening, and at these the repre- sentatives on the parochial authorities will be elected by show of hands, that is unless a poll is demanded, in which event the usual routine of an election will have to be observed. At Padgate the all-important question of sewering the district will be discussed, and as an extensive scheme is proposed, the matter will be followed with interest.

At Stockton Heath no burning question is immedi- ately on the carpet though it is thought that the vexed question of tree-planting will be again introduced. Opposition to the return of the sitting Councillors is promised and in certain quarters there are dark hints at a clean sweep and a complete change in the complex- ion of the Council. Opposition is also threatened to the return of Messrs. Whitley and Hallows, the sit ting representatives on the District Council and Board of Guardians,and it is understood that Messrs. Mynekyme and C. W. Davenport will seek to be returned.

"The Beauty of Bath."

After a remarkable run of prosperity at, the Aldwycli and Hicks Theatres, "The Beauty of Bath" comes to the Shakespeare Theatre,Liverpool,on Monday next for a fortnight. Liverpool playgoers are lortunate in being the first provincial audience to have the opportunity of witnessing Charles Frohman's dainty production, with Miss Ellaline Terriss and Mr. Seymour Hicks in their original parts. The play is distinguished by magnificent scenery, exquisite dresses, and ear-haunt- ing music, and has the added merit, so rare in musical pieces, of containing an interesting story. The theme of the story is "David Garrick," and the scene is laid in the period of powdered wigs and knee- breeches. The " book " has been written by Mr. Seymour Hicks and Mr. Cosmo Hamilton, the lyrics by Mr. Charles H. Taylor and the music by Mr. Herbert E. Haines. No more popular engagement could have been entered into by the Shakespeare Theatre manage- ment for the inauguration of the regular spring season. At each performance the " Bath Buns" will appear. Miss Ellaline Terriss will sing "Shy Ann" and " Hyacinth" while Mr. Seymour Hicks will sing his famous topical song "Mr. Chamberlain."

===== 0002601_19070302_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE. THE IMPROVED STATE OF TRADE. Manufacturers and High Rates.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce was held at the Town Hall on Thursday, Councilor J. Fulton Smith presiding. There were also present :—Captain G. R. Crosfield, Messrs. C. E. Parker, John Fairclough, o.Lofthouse,H.Kingdon,and Alderman Bennett (secretary).

THE WORK OF THE YEA.R. WILL WARRINGTON HAVE A DOCK?

The SECRETARY in his annual report said that with regard to the Warrington Dock and Lay-by, he had reported to the Council that the Ship Canal Company had arranged to rent from Messrs. R. A. Naylor Limited a portion of the shed which they were building near the lay-by with a view to providing the desired facilities for dealing with local traffic, and he was requested to arrange a meeting between the committee, Mr. W. Johnson, who had canvassed many of the local traders, and representatives of several shipping companies. At the suggestion of Mr. Johnson' the meeting was deferred until the arrangements at the lay-by were complete. He (the secretary) accompanied the President, on behalf of the Rivers Committee of the Corporation, on a deputa- tion to the Manchester Ship Canal Company to discuss the question of the Warrington dock, the term allowed to the Corporation to commence the construction of which having expired on April 9th. They asked the Company to allow an extension of time allowed by the agreement, Mr.Bythell, the Chairman of the Company, said the spoil which was taken from the river and placed on the site of the proposed dock was taken out of the river at the request of the Corporation in accordance with the agreement as to dredging, and he did not know of any alternative place in which to deposit it. Mr. Hunter (the engineer)added that in the next four or five years, at the present rate of progress, the whole of Arpley Meadows would probably be covered with silt from the river. In reply to further representations by the deputation, Mr. Bythell said that whilst the Canal Company did not think they ought to tie their hands in reference to the matter, they were quite disposed to , view any &finite suggestions sympathetically, and to negotiate with the Corporation at any time for the con- struction of a dock. He added that they did not at I present, propose to tip the spoil to the extreme margin lof the old river, add suggested that the Corporation might commence with the construction of a piled wharf in the bed of it, deepening it by dredging, and gradually extending towards St. James' Church. This might, he said, be done at a comparatively small cost, and the Canal Company would be glad to assist the Corporation in the matter.

At a later date a letter was received from Mr. E. L. Fleming intimating that the Ship Canal Company had taken over the warehouse, built near the the transporter by R. A. Naylor Ltd., and proposed to light the lay-by with electricity, pave the road communicating there- with, and provide a loading platform, and suggesting that the Chamber should ask the principal coasting companies whether they intended to stop their vessels at IVarrington for local goods at regular dates, and failing their compliance, to invite other companies to start a line of vessels from Warrington to the coast and some continental ports. After some discussion, it was decided that the Secretary should write to the principal steamship companies affected asking them to place Warrington on their sailing bills and to make it a regular port of call. This was done and replies more or less favourable were received from Fisher, Renwick and Co. (London, Southampton Steamers), the Dublin and Manchester Steamship Company, F. H. Powell and Co. (through Mr. E. L. Fleming), and from Herbert Watson and Co. (Genoa and Leghorn). He was instructed to communicate again with Messrs. Watson and Co. asking them whether they had made any progress as to the proposed arrangments for dealing with local Mediterranean traffic and especially if they would give any deflaite information as to rates, &a. They replibd to the effect that the matter was still receiving their consideration and that they would write the Chamber further at a later stage. It was also reported to the Council that the Ship Canal Company were introducing a Bill in the next Session qf Parlia- ment empowering them, amongst other things, to acquire additional spoil beds in the vicinity of Walton Arches and the railway, and to pump five millions of water from the estuary at Eastham in the canal. and from that or any other reach into the reaches beyond it. The Council trusted that, with the acquisi- tion of further land, the company would cease to use the site of the Warrington Dock for the silt dredged from the river. They hoped that, in reference to the pumping, proper precautions would be taken to prevent any possible further depletion of the quantity of water flowing down the river, and any deterioration in the quality of same for boiler purposes. They were glad to report that the electric light had been installed at the lay-by, that LA. Naylor Ltd., had landed several cargoes of timber there, and they were carefully watching all developments with a view to assisting as much as possible in the utilization of the lay-by and the eventual construction or a dock. The SECRETARY also gave a report of thE proceedings of the Congress of Chambers of Commerce of the Empire and after referring at length to preference to the Colonies, he said the ultimate resolution passed was the following :—" That this Congress urges upon his Majesty's Government, and upon the Government of the Colonies, the appointment of an Imperial Council to consider questions of Imperial interest, especially those tending to promote international ttade." With regard to the finances, Alderman BENNE-TT reported that a debit balance of £4 19s. 3d. had been reduced to £1 19s. ad.

The CHAIRMAN in moving the adoption of the report said that since the last annual meeting it had been generally acknowledged that trade had immensely improved in the country, and that prospects were very good at the present day. There was now a disposition to go into more business questions from the Parlia- mentary side than had been the case hitherto, it was interesting to see that Commissions had been formed with reference to shipping rings and railway rates. These were vital points which affected trade generally, and it was one of the elements of business being successful or otherwise when they had to contend with a bounty-fed foreign competition, as against the heavy transit rates manufacturers had to face in this country. Another point which militated very much against manufacturers in this country was the high local 'rates. There was a great cry for economy, but it seemed to be almost an impossibility to economise, because there were so many demands made for branches of municipal Government which did not directly come in the category of trade; such as sanitary improvements and education, which were adding materially to the out- lay of manufacturers. Atost people would like to see the rates reduced, but the only alternative which could be offered was a higher assessment, and that would produce about the same result as far as the manufac- turers were concerned. Although there had not been so much disturbance about them, some little economies had been arranged by the Council during the year which perhaps did not appear on the surface. The condition of the Chamber of Commerce had undoubtedly improved as compared with what it was a few years ago and if more M the manufacturers of the town would be repre- sented upon it, the results would be mutually beneficial. LOPTHOUSE seconded.

Mr. PA.EtKER in supporting said that if he had been present at the Council meeting he should have suggested the cutting out of a great deal of the matter about preferential tariffs, fie did not see that it was a very necessary part of such a report to have inserted the suggestion about a levy all round from the colonies, in as much as the suggestion did not take any definite form and did not emanate from the Council. The SECRETNIIY : It is simply leading up to the other suggestion which was adopted. Tan report was adopted.

===== 0002601_19070302_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATIONAL PROBLF,NIS.

Mr. KINGDON, as the representative on the Education Committee, presented a report on the work done during 1903 by that authority. Dealing with Elementary Education, he sAid that no one would wish to go back to the old system of payment by results, but still it must be considered that a large percentage of the children when they left school possessed a remarkable lack of initiative and not too much sound knowledge. 'Slang people only took sufficient interest in education to grumble once a year when the annual rate was announced ; those who had studied the question closely were of opinion that to secure the best results they must face a considerable increase in the number of fully qualified teachers on their teaching staffs. In some places a teacher was expected to deal with clascies of from 70 to 80 at a time ; and while it might be possible to take such a number for the manual exercises,good results could not be expected I when anyone, either man or woman, was expected to educate that number simultaneously. Here the function of a Chamber of Commerce should not be to demand a reduction in the money spent on education, but rather that whatever sums were voted were spent efficiently and well. Probably an even more serious problem was how best to secure the attendance at evening classes of those who left school at about the age of 13, in other words, how were they going to ensure that their educa- tion should be continued just at the age when a child was most receptive and his character in after life most likely to be formed ? He doubted if anything less than an Act of Parliament rendering attendance at evening schools compulsory on the part of every child leaving the primary schools would ever give that impetus to educa- tion amongst the lower classes which was necessary if we were to catch up in any short time the leeway that we undoubtedly had made compared with the progress in that respect of other great nations. They all knew the tremendous lever in favour of education that a country having universal military service possessed, because it was then custom- ary to remit a year of such service provided a certain educational level had been attained, and that applied to other classes besides the lower ones, and cases had been known where young men would prefer to leave the country, who had failed to attain that necessary standard, rather than Lice the ignominy, for such it was regarded, of having to serve the whole period. Failing a mandate from Westminster, it behoved them to see to what extent the commercial community could assist in that direction, and a number of emnloyers thron.hout the country were awakening to the fact that tie continue I

_ OTHER ELECTIONS.

Mr. 0. Lofthouse was elected vice-president, Mr. William Skinner was re-elected treasurer, and Messrs. T. and J. L Tunstall were re-appointed hon. auditors. ALTERATION OF RULES.

The meeting agreed to an alteration of the rule which provides that the Mayor and M.P. for the Borough should be ex-oflicio members of the Council, to the effect that they should also be hon.. vice-presidents.

A vote of thinks to the Chairman for presiding and the Mayor for the use of the room concluded the meeting.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

' [By " Ovalist.") Granted a continuance of the present fine weather and the Warrington crowd will flock to Wilderspool in it thousands this afternoon. e visit of the Broughton Rangers is always looked rward to, and as a rule they give the hotnesters a close game. There has been so little football in the town of late that the match is being eagerly anticipated. On the occasion of the first meeting between the teams at Broughton, the Rangers won a poor game by 11 points to 2 points. Since then Warrington have had the much better record, and with a chance of getting into the "first four" they should improve it at the expense of the visitors. Both teams are expected to be si full strength, and the contest should, therefore, be worth seeing.

There is not quite the same confidence in the War- rington men that there was a week or two ag), for the defeat at the hands of Oldhatn was 'a rude rebuff. Moreover the team has not been showing that com- bination and dash which proved irresistible in the first half of the season. Still, the injuries to some of the best backs may be accountable for this. Fish has been much missed during the past three months and it is to be hoped that he will prove himself in form once more to-day. His tactics alone mean a lot to the team, but s )mething more than Napoleonic general- ship is required from him What the crowd likes is some fast, tricky running completed by a try, and it will be Interesting to see how much of the " irrepressible's " power has been left him as the result of such a long spell of inactivity.

Another important change this week is the sub- stitution of Tilley for Berry at full back. The young custodian has not defended in the most reliable fashion for some time, so the Widnes man gets the chance he has probably been longing for. The selected terns are :

Warrington: Tilley, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson, and Brooks, threequarters ; Lees and Jenkins, half backs ; Boardman, Preston, Heath, Thomas, Shugars, and Harmer forwards.

Broughton Rangers : Barlow, full back ; Hogz (eapt.), Harry and Harris, threequarters ; S, James, and Flynn, half backs; Ruddick, Beetham. Whitehead, Mottram, Darlison, Clampitt, and Grainey (to be selected from).

The Warrington men vill be anxious to show that the result of the Lancashire Cup Final was all wrong. Fish owes the Rangers something, for they broke his arm on that occasion. It is rather curious that his -first serious match is against the same team. A certain Broughton forward will have to mind his " p's and q's " and a few other things to-day or he will find himself in the wars. •

I fancy their chance against Halifax more than Runcorn's for the simple reason„that Hanson-lane is one of the grounds the Wirepullers almost always do well upon. Last season they halved the points the score being 10 —lO, and the season before they made some amends for the cup final defeat by winning 10-0. As a matter of fact Halifax have not beaten Warrington since the year they robbed them of the cup in the final tie at Weaste. Therefore I am hopeful of some result which will pat the Warrington men yell in the running for the top four. The Combination match at Broughton to-day should produce a keen contest, but the fact that Warrington have lost the championship for this season robs it of some interest. THE WAR MEMORIAL. Letters From General Buller and

Colonel Adam.

The following letters have been received this week at the Town Hall:

High Legh Hall, Keutsford. February 22nd, 1907. Detar Sir, - I cannot leave High Leh without writing to ask you to convey to the Mayor and. Corpration of Warrington my thanks for the magnifi- cent reception they arranged for me yesterday and my appreciation of the excellence of all the arrange- ments. Everything was so comfortable and all went without a bitch.

Nly thanks are also particularly due to yourself, and I may add to the kind people of Warrington who gave me a reception which I shall never forget. I do not know Mr. Roberts' address or Jrwould write • to him personally ; my I ask you to thank him for me. Yours faithfully, REDVERS BULLER. The Town Clerk, Warrington. Bldckdown Farnborough, Pieter's Hill Day, 1907. Dear Mr. Mayor, —I feel that I must take the earliest possible opportunity, after the historic events which have passed in the last few days at Warrington, to write and express to you the very coerdial thanks and gratitude of all the officers, non-commissioned officers and men of my battalion to whom you extended i SO kindly a welcome and such thorough Lancashire =1 hospitality during the ever memorable and all too brief visit. We felt at our departure that we left behind us in the North many kind friends and warm hearts. We have been much touched and gratified by the fact that the townspeople of Warrington now really look upon us as their regiment, and I luope the - result may be that we shall succeed in inducing many of your young men to join with us in striving to uphold the grand history and untarnished name of the 40th and 82nd Regiments, now known as the South L3nca- shires. We have a great 'heritage handed dowu to us by the gallant men who have gone before us : "We are a people yet Tho' all men else their nobler dreams forget,. Confused by brainless mobs and lawless Powers. • 4 Thank Him who isled as here, and roughly set His Briton in blown seas and storming showers, Wehave a voice, with which to pay the debt Of boundless love, and reverence, and regret To those great men who fought, and kept it ours." I cannot recollect a single hitch or untoward ° incident of any kind throughout the late festivities; and I feel that it is almost impossible for me to per- *.,4 sonally thank all who contributed to such a successful issue.

To the Memorial Committee (not forgetting the splendid work done by the yirorkingmen'a Committee), to the Borough Treasurer and Surveyor, to the Town Clerk, upon whose sboulders the chief burden seems- 31 to have fallen, to our kind entertainers, not forgetting the ladies and gentlemen who were at such pains to make the Friday evening concert a success, nor those who on Sunday afternoon so generously patronized the sacred concert given by the Band in aid of the Memorial Funds to many others too numerous to mention in this brief letter, and last, but not least, Mr. Mayor, to your good self, our warmest acknowledg- ments are due, and I beg you to take this letter, imperfect and inadequate although it seems, as an earnest of our inner feelings. We shall not soon forget our welcome, whilst I know that our gallant comrades of the Ist Battalion will be as proud as we are of all the events connected with the unveiling of the Regimental Memorial. I trust, Sir, that you may be pleased ti make known to all who welcomed us, in as public a manner as may be desirable, how muck, beholden we are to them; and subscribe myself Yours gratefully and sincerely, F. A. ADAM. His Worship the Mayor of Warrington.

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB. Annual Meeting. RESIGNATION OF MR. E. PICKAVANCE.

It was a highly successful season which the memberc• of the Warrington Cycling Club had the satisfaction of reviewing at their annual meeting held at the Pattec Arms Hotel on Thursday evening. There was, however,. one great cause of regret, and that was the resignation from the secretaryship, after many years' devoted service, of Mr. E. Pickavance. The meeting, which was well attended, was presided over by Mr. G. Bowman (captain)._ - -

Before the business commenced, the CHAIRMAN made, a sympathetic allusion to the absence of Mr. Picka- vance who, he said, had been a conspicuous figure at the meetings for many years. As the members were aware, he had just passed through a very serious illness, but they were pleased to hear that he was convalescent, and hoped he would soon be restored to health. (Hear, hear.)

Mr. E. Pickavance had, as usual, prepared a most interesting report and it Was read by Mr. F.Fieldhouse, who acted as Secretary pro tem. It detailed the doings of the Club in interesting language and was listened to with pleasure. The attendance at Club runs has aver- aged twenty for Saturdays and eighteen for Tuesday nights. The attendance prizes have been won by Messrs. H. W. Whittle, G. Bowman and Sergeant Major Delmage. The event of the season undoubtedly was the great effort made by the Club in organisin g the Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival in aid of the Warrington Infirmary Extension Fund. The financial result was superb, as after meeting all- expenses a cheque for .2410 14s. 9d. was forwarded to the Treasurer of the Infirmary. The club membership now stands at 112 active and 11 honorary members, which at once proves the popularity and strong position of the club. The Treasurer's balance sheet shows that after meeting all expenses we have a handsome balance in hand, and the Chancellor of our Exchequer is to be complimented on the •splendid manner in which he handles our finances. The Treasurer (Mr. F. Garnett) reported that there was a balance in hand of £36 Bs. 4d. Votes of thanks were accorded to all the retiring officers, a special tribute being paid to the services a Mr. Pickavance and general regret being expressed at his resignation.

THE NEW OFFICERS. With great cordiality the Club unanimously re-elected Mr. John Naylor to the presidency. The Mayor was elected a vice-president. Mr. F. Fieldhouse was elected captain, and Major Deimage sub-captain. Mr. GEORGE H. Smmi was elected to the secretary- ship and in returning thanks said it woald be a difficult matter to follow Mr. Pickavance whose resignation they deeply deplored. He would do his utmost to. further the interests of the Club and maintain its status- and prosperity. Mr. PICKAVANCE was elected an active vice-president in place of the late Dr. Kisby. Mr. F. GARNETT was, re-elected treasurer and the following were appointed the committee :—Messrs. G. Bowman, W. Fl. Haitian, E. Pickavance, J. L. Davenport,. A. Booth, W. Taylor, and J. Houghton. Messrs. Pickavance and Booth and the Secretary were delegates to the N.C.U. and Messrs. W. Litton ind J. Podmore were elected auditors. There was a general feeling that the 21st anniversary of the club should be fittingly recognized and a social function will probably be arranged.

Unsuccessful Business Venture. At the Bankruptcy Court yesterday, David Artnstrong, fitter, Clegg-street,appeared for his public examination in bankruptcy. Six years ago the bank- rupt started a grocery business with ;ell capital borrowed from his sons. His wife had sole charge of the shop. The failure was attributed to the fact that debtor did not do a large enough business to make a profit. The liabilities amounted to .tll4 and the asset& to S.:B 104. The examination was closed.

TO THB PILBOTORS OF TUN APPLIIITON LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, I take this earliest opportunity of conveying to you my sincere thanks for again electing me as your repre- sentative on the Cheshire County Council without opposition. I deeply appreciate the honour you have conferred upon me end can assere you that I shall always endeavour to carry out my duties in the beat interests of the County generally and especially of the Appleton I have the honour to be, Ladies ana Gentlemen, Your obedient servant, hOGISR CHARLTON PARR. March 2nd, 1907. LAINUMbHIRR COON Y COUNCIL, - 'TO THE .' ELEC ORS OF THE WIN WICK DIVISION. LADIE9 AND GENTLItMEN. At the request of a number of influential ratepayers in this Division I have consented to offer myself as a Candidate at the Jorthooming Mention. 1 have been a resident in the Division pm:Cr:tally all my life and am well-known to most of you. ray intimate knowledge of the District (and of the people) will enable me to put forward its requirements. For many years I was a Member of the Burtonwood School Board and since tt.e abolition of School Board' have been continuously a achool X eger and have taken an active interest in educational work, I h ,ve been a Member of the Burtonwood Parish Council since its formation, having for the last three years tilled the important postion of Chairman of the Council. Having served for nine years on the Rural District Council, I claim to have a fair knowledge of Hibliway and Sanitary matters. I have also been a Guardian of the Po,r for nine veers and have servi d on all the t ommittees and for two years hsve had the honour of being Chairman of the House Committee The great experience which I have thus gained will the b tter enable me to effectively represent your interests on the County tiotr.cil, while the fact that I am myself alto a Tenant Farmer will assure the Riectors of my interest and care in all agricultural matters. If you should honour me by electing me as your Repretentative I can assure you that I shall give proper attention to the duties of the office and I will devote the beat of my abilities to the services of this important Division. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Your Obedient Servant, RICHARD FORt-HAW. Buernwood, 27th irtibruary, 1907.

A week after this we took possession, and I was charmed with the privacy of our new residence after the discomforts of the flat which we had occupied since our marriage. I found it so much pleasanter to have a house all to oneself, and "oo feel that within the four walls of our abode no one had the right of entry but ourselves. On the other hand, I was somewhat annoyed by Laura's frequent references to the finger on the blind and by her continued nervousness. She was bound to admit that there were no traces of a mysterious occupant now, but she persisted that she could not possibly have been mistaken in what she had seen. As usual in a London street, we had no com- munications with the neighbours on either side of us, but they certainly lived up to the character given them by the house agent's clerk, being quiet, and therefore presumably respectable. It was not till we had been in residence two months that they both came under our notice—strangely enough on the same day. lam a barrister by profession, and wsia away (1....:na lintirct On the ft... 37 in D

"THE VOICE 0 F LABOUR." ON SATURDAY, DIASROEi I6TH, A NEW FEATURE will be introduced to readers of the" Warrington Examiner," which should prove of special attraction and value to members of the INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY, the PRO- GRESSIVE LABOUR LIIAGUR, the LIDO" REPRESENTATION 001111ITTER, the SOOIAL- ISTS, the TRADE{ UNION SO3IRTIR3 and all others who take an inteliigent interest la the advancement and future of the Democracy. UNDER THE TITLE OF "THE VOICE OF LABOUR" a column will be set apart each week for con- tributions upon social topics. Many of the most enlightened Labourists In the town have pro mised assistance in making the column a real " round table" for free discussion. Contributions will be welcomed from all sources, arm must be addressed to : " JOHN WORKMAN," "EXAMINER '' OFFICE, • WARRINGTON. PLEA3B EOTB THAT— "THE VOICE OF LABOUR" WILL COMMENCE IN THE "EXAMINER,' ON MARCH 16TH,

WARRINGTON Coming Events. The annual meeting of the Boro ugh of Warrington Incorporated Permanent Benefit Building Society will be held at Messrs. Suttons' office, Legh-street, on Wednesday evening. On Tuesday evening a debate on the land question will be held in the Providence schoolroom, Stockton Heath, under the auspices of the Stockton Heath Liberal Association. Captain Crosfield will address the Warrington Junior Liberals on Monday evening on "The Haldane administration of the War office." The meeting will be held at Gregory's Cafe. We would remind our readers of the concert in the Parr Hall on March 20th at which Madame Clara Butt and other well known artistes will appear. The Beautiful Warrington Society will this after- noon plant trees in Morley-street, Manchester-road. The proceedings will commence at three o'clock. The fourteenth anniversary of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society will be celebrated on Sunday. The Rev. J. Yonge will preside, and an address will be delivered by Mr. John Harrison. Messrs. Crosfield's Band will give selections. Personal. The Mayor was the guest of Colonel Adam and officers of the Ist and 2nd Battalion Prince of Wales's Volunteers South Lancashire Regiment, at a dinner at the Adelphi Hotel, Liverpool, on Saturday evening, several of the members of the Warrington War Memorial Executive Committee being also present including the Chairman (Mr. H. Roberts). The Mayor's health was proposed in felicitous terms by Colonel Adam, who presided, and received with musical honours, and when his Worship left the room to catch the train to Warrington he was carried shoulder high by the officers to the front of the Hotel where he had a hearty send-off. Infirmary Concert. We are pleased to announce that the annual concert in aid of the Infirmary organized by the Workingmen's Committee realized the handsome sum of £56 Gs. 6d. net. Ragged School and Mission. On Sunday evening Mr. H. Pilcher Wright gave an excellent address on " The Book of Esther." The story was illustrated with lantern slides and was very instructive. Miss May Harris sweetly sang an illus- trated hymn. Mr. Blakemore accampanied and Mr. Frank Owen was the laternist. The committee beg to gratefully acknowledge the splendid tea given by Mrs. Crosfield to 300 of the scholars. _ . _
